“How long,” she queried listlessly, “before﻿—before you’ll know?”
“Perhaps immediately. The only chance is to get that bullet out at once﻿—if there’s still time for it.”
She followed him into the building, past a desk where a white-clad girl regarded her curiously, and up an elevator. He led her into a small office.
“Sit here,” he said gently, and disappeared.
She sat dully in the chair he had indicated, and minutes passed. She made no attempt to think; the long, cataclysmic night had exhausted her powers. She simply sat and suffered; the deep scratches of fingernails burned in the flesh of her back, her cheek pained from the violent slap, and her head and jaw ached from that first blow, the one that had knocked her unconscious last evening. But these twinges were minor; they were merely physical, and the hurts of the demon had struck far deeper than any physical injury. The damage to her spirit was by all odds the more painful; it numbed her mind and dulled her thoughts, and she simply sat idle and stared at the blank wall.
She had no conception of the interval before Dr. Horker returned. He entered quietly, and began rinsing his hands at a basin in the corner.

===Chinese===
“要多久，”她无精打采地问，“才能——才能知道结果？”
“也许马上就知道。唯一的生机是立刻取出那颗子弹——如果还来得及的话。”
她跟着他进了大楼，经过一张桌子——桌后的白衣姑娘好奇地打量她——又上了电梯。他把她领进一间小办公室。
“坐这儿，”他温和地说，随即不见了。
她呆坐在他指的那把椅子里，几分钟过去了。她无意去思考；那漫长、天崩地裂的一夜已耗尽了她的心力。她只是坐着受苦；背上肉里指甲抓出的深深伤痕火辣辣地疼，脸颊上被狠掴的地方作痛，头和下巴因最初那一击而隐隐发酸——就是昨晚把她打昏的那一击。可这些刺痛都算小事；它们只是皮肉之苦，而恶魔的伤害扎得远比任何外伤深。她精神上受的创痛才是最痛的；它麻痹了她的头脑，钝化了她的思绪，她只是干坐着，望着空白的墙。
霍克医生回来之前过了多久，她毫无概念。他悄声进来，在角落的洗手盆前冲洗双手。

===Original===
“Is it over?” she asked listlessly.
“Not even begun,” he responded. “However, it isn’t too late. He’ll be ready in a moment or so.”
“I wish it were over,” she murmured. “One way or the other.”
“I too!” said the Doctor. “With all my heart, I wish it were over! If there were anyone within call who could handle it, I’d turn it to him gladly. But there isn’t!”
He moved again toward the door, leaning out and glancing down the hall.

===Chinese===
“完了吗？”她恹恹地问。
“还没开始呢，”他答道。“不过不算太晚。他马上就准备好了。”
“我巴不得它已经完了，”她喃喃道。“不管哪种结果。”
“我也一样！”医生说。“我全心全意盼着它了结！要是近旁有谁能接手这活儿，我乐意让给他。可没有这样的人！”
他又朝门口走去，探出身子往走廊那头张望。

===Original===
“You stay here,” he admonished her. “Don’t try to find us; I want no interruptions, no matter what enters that mind of yours!”
“You needn’t worry,” she said soberly. “I’m not fool enough for that.” She leaned wearily back in the chair, closing her eyes. A long interval passed; she was vaguely surprised to see the Doctor still standing in the doorway when she opened her eyes. She had fancied him already in the midst of his labor.
“What will you do?” she asked.
“About what?”
“I mean what sort of operation will it need? Probing or what?”

===Chinese===
“你待在这儿，”他叮嘱她。“别想来找我们；我不要任何打扰，不管你那脑子里冒出什么念头！”
“用不着担心，”她正色道。“我还没傻到那份上。”她疲惫地靠进椅背，闭上眼睛。漫长的间隔过去；再睁眼时，见医生还站在门口，她隐隐有些诧异。她还以为他早已在手术台前忙开了。
“你要做什么？”她问。
“做什么——指什么？”
“我是说需要做什么样的手术？探查还是别的？”

===Original===
“Oh,” he said. “I’ll have to trephine him. Must get that bullet.”
“What’s that﻿—trephine?”
He glanced down the hall. “They’re ready,” he said, and turned to go. At the door he paused. “Trephining is to open a little door in the skull. If your devil is in his head, we’ll have it out along with the bullet.”
His footsteps receded down the hall.

===Chinese===
“哦，”他说。“我得给他做环钻术。必须取出那颗子弹。”
“那是什么——环钻术？”
他朝走廊那头望了一眼。“他们准备好了，”他说着转身要走。到了门口他又停住。“环钻术就是在颅骨上开一扇小门。要是你的魔鬼真在他脑袋里，我们就连它带子弹一起取出来。”
他的脚步声在走廊尽头渐渐远去。

## XVI Possessed / 十六 附体
===Original===
Pat stared at the intruder in a mingling of embarrassment, perplexity, and indignation. She felt her cheeks reddening as the latter emotion gained the dominance of her mood.
“Well!” she snapped. “What do you want?”
“I thought I’d walk home with you,” Mueller said amiably.
“Walk home with me! Please explain that!” She grasped the arm of Nicholas Devine, who had risen angrily at the interruption. “Sit down, Nick, I know the fellow.”
“So should he,” said Mueller. “Sure; I’ll explain. I’m on a job for Dr. Horker.”
“Spying on me for him, I suppose!” taunted the girl.

===Chinese===
帕特盯着这位不速之客，窘迫、困惑与恼怒交织在一起。当恼怒渐渐占了上风，她感到自己双颊发热泛红。
“哼！”她厉声道。“你想干什么？”
“我想陪你走回家，”缪勒和气地说。
“陪我走回家！这话怎么讲！”她抓住尼古拉斯·德文的手臂——他已因这打断而愠怒地站起。“坐下，尼克，我认识这家伙。”
“他也该认识我，”缪勒说。“行，我解释。我在替霍克医生办事。”
“替他监视我，我猜！”姑娘讥讽道。

===Original===
“No. Not on you.”
“He means on me,” said Nick soberly. “You can’t blame him, Pat. And perhaps you had better go home; we’ve finished here. There’s nothing more we can do or say.”
“Very well,” she said, her voice suddenly softer. “In a moment, Nick.” She turned to Mueller. “Would you mind telling me why you waited until now to interfere? We’ve been here two hours, you know.”
“Sure I’ll tell you. I got no orders to interfere, that’s why.”
“Then why did you?” queried Pat tartly.
“I didn’t until I saw him there”﻿—he nodded at Nick﻿—“put his arms around you. Then I figured, having no orders, it was time to use my own judgment.”

===Chinese===
“不。不是监视你。”
“他是说监视我，”尼克正色道。“你怪不得他，帕特。也许你还是回家的好；我们的事谈完了。再没什么可做、可说的了。”
“好吧，”她说，声音忽然柔和下来。“再等一下，尼克。”她转向缪勒。“能不能告诉我，你为什么等到现在才出面搅局？你知道的，我们在这儿待了两个钟头了。”
“当然可以。没人命令我出面，就这么回事。”
“那你为什么出面？”帕特尖刻地问。
“本来我是不会出面的，直到我看见他”——他朝尼克点点头——“把胳膊搭上你。我寻思，既然没有命令，那就该轮到我自己的判断做主了。”

===Original===
“If any!” sniffed the girl. She turned again to Nick; her face softened, became very tender. “Honey,” she murmured huskily, “I guess it’s goodbye now. I’ll be fighting with you; you know that.”
“I know that,” he echoed, looking down into her eyes. “I’m almost happy, Pat.”
“When’ll you go?” she whispered in tones inaudible to Mueller.
“I don’t know,” he answered, his voice unchanged. “I’ll have to make some sort of preparations﻿—and I don’t want you to know.”
She nodded. She gazed at him a moment longer with tear-bright eyes. “Goodbye, Nick,” she whispered. She rose on tiptoe, and kissed him very lightly on his lips, then turned and walked quickly away, with Mueller following behind.
She walked on, ignoring him until he halted beside her at the crossing of the Drive. Then she gave him a cold glance.

===Chinese===
“要是你有那玩意儿的话！”姑娘嗤了一声。她又转向尼克；她的脸柔和下来，变得极为温柔。“亲爱的，”她嗓音沙哑地低语，“我想现在该道别了。我会和你并肩作战；这你知道。”
“这我知道，”他应道，低头凝视她的眼睛。“我几乎觉得幸福，帕特。”
“你什么时候走？”她用缪勒听不见的声调低问。
“不知道，”他答道，语气平平。“我得做些准备——而我不想让你知道。”
她点点头。她噙着泪光的眼睛又凝望了他片刻。“再见，尼克，”她轻声说。她踮起脚尖，极轻地在他唇上一吻，随即转身快步走开，缪勒跟在后面。
她径自走着，不理会他，直到他在大道交叉口停下步子，她才冷冷瞥了他一眼。

===Original===
“Why is Dr. Carl having him watched?” she asked.
Mueller shrugged. “The ins and outs of this case are too much for me,” he said. “I do what I’m paid to do.”
“You’re not watching him now.”
“Nope. Seemed like the Doctor would think it was more important to get you home.”
“You’re wasting your time,” she said irritably as the lights changed and they stepped into the street. “I was going home anyway.”
“Well, now you got company all the way.” Mueller’s voice was placid.

===Chinese===
“卡尔医生为什么派人监视他？”她问。
缪勒耸耸肩。“这案子的曲曲折折我搞不懂，”他说。“拿人钱财，替人办事。”
“你现在可没在监视他。”
“没。看样子医生觉得送你回家更要紧。”
“你在白费工夫，”信号灯变换、两人走进街道时，她烦躁地说。“我本来就要回家。”
“这下好了，一路上都有人做伴。”缪勒的声气平平。

===Original===
The girl sniffed contemptuously, and strode silently along. The other’s presence irritated her; she wanted time and solitude to consider the amazing story Nicholas Devine had given her. She wanted to analyze her own feelings, and most of all she wanted just a place of privacy to cry out her misery. For now the loss of Nicholas Devine had changed from a fortunate escape to a tragedy, and liar, madman, or devil, she wanted him terribly, with all the power of her tense little heart. So she moved as swiftly as she could, ignoring the silent companionship of Mueller.
They reached her home; the light in the living room window was evidence that the bridge game was still in progress. She mounted the steps, Mueller watching her silently from the walk; she fumbled for her key.
Suddenly she snapped her handbag shut; she couldn’t face her mother and the two spinster Brocks and elderly, inquisitive Carter Henderson. They’d suggest that she cut into the game, and they’d argue if she refused, and she couldn’t play bridge now! She glanced at the impassive Mueller, turned and crossed the strip of lawn to Dr. Horker’s residence, where the light still glowed in the library, and rang the bell. She saw the figure on the sidewalk move away as the shadow of the Doctor appeared on the lighted square of the door.
“Hello,” boomed the Doctor amiably. “Come in.”
Pat stalked into the library and threw herself angrily into Dr. Horker’s particular chair. The other grinned, and chose another place.
“Well,” he said, “what touched off the fuse this time?”

===Chinese===
姑娘轻蔑地嗤了一声，闷头大步向前。这个人的存在让她恼火；她需要时间和独处，来咀嚼尼古拉斯·德文讲给她的那个惊人事。她想理一理自己的感情，而最要紧的是，她需要一处没人打扰的地方，好痛哭一场她的悲伤。如今，失去尼古拉斯·德文已从一场万幸的解脱变成一出悲剧；他是撒谎者也罢，疯子也罢，魔鬼也罢，她都要他，要得发疯，用她那颗紧绷的小小心脏的全部气力。于是她只顾尽快赶路，全当身边的缪勒不存在。
到了她家门口；客厅窗里的灯光表明桥牌局还在进行。她登上台阶，缪勒从步道上默然望着；她摸索钥匙。
突然她啪地合上手袋；她没法去面对母亲、布罗克家那一对老小姐姐妹，还有好事的老卡特·亨德森。他们会拉她入局，她一拒绝他们还会劝，可她现在哪有心思打桥牌！她瞥了一眼面无表情的缪勒，转身穿过那片草地，走向霍克医生的宅邸——书房的灯还亮着——按响了门铃。医生的身影出现在门内亮堂堂的方格玻璃上时，她看见人行道上那具人影退开了。
“哈罗，”医生和蔼地瓮声说道。“进来。”
帕特大步走进书房，气冲冲地一屁股坐进霍克医生的专用椅。对方咧嘴一笑，另拣了个位子。
“怎么样，”他说，“这回又是什么点着了引线？”

===Original===
“Why are you spying on my friends?” snapped the girl. “By what right?”
“So he’s spotted Mueller, eh? That lad’s diabolically clever, Pat﻿—and I mean diabolic.”
“That’s no answer!”
“So it isn’t,” agreed the Doctor. “Say it’s because I’m acting *in loco parentis*.”
“And *in loco* is as far as you’ll get, Dr. Carl, if you’re going to spy on me!”
“On you?” he said mildly. “Who’s spying on you?”

===Chinese===
“你为什么监视我的朋友？”姑娘厉声说。“你凭什么？”
“这么说他认出缪勒了，嗯？那小子鬼精鬼精的，帕特——我说的是魔鬼般的精。”
“这不算回答！”
“确实不算，”医生附和道。“就算我是以*代理家长*的身份行事吧。”
“可要是你还敢监视我，卡尔医生，你这*代理*也就代理到头了！”
“监视你？”他温和地说。“谁监视你了？”

===Original===
“On us, then!”
“Or on us?” queried the Doctor. “I set Mueller to watch the Devine lad. Have you by some mischance broken your promise to me?”
Pat flushed. She had forgotten that broken promise; the recollection of it suddenly took the wind from her sails, placed her on the defensive.
“All right,” she said defiantly. “I did; I admit it. Does that excuse you?”
“Perhaps it helps to explain my actions, Pat. Don’t you understand that I’m trying to protect you? Do you think I hired Mueller out of morbid curiosity, or professional interest in the case? Times aren’t so good that I can throw money away on such whims.”
“I don’t need any protection. I can take care of myself!”

===Chinese===
“那就是监视我们！”
“还是监视‘我们’？”医生问道。“我是派缪勒盯着德文那小子。你该不会阴差阳错，又违背了对我的承诺吧？”
帕特脸红了。她把毁约这茬给忘了；这记猛然想起，顿时让她泄了气，落到了守势。
“好吧，”她倔强地说。“我是去了；我承认。这就能开脱你吗？”
“它也许能解释我的举动，帕特。你不明白我是在设法保护你吗？你以为我雇缪勒是出于病态的好奇，或是这个案子的职业兴味？年景还没好到我能把银子往这种心血来潮上乱扔的地步。”
“我不需要什么保护。我自己照顾得了自己！”

===Original===
“So I noticed,” said the Doctor dryly. “You gave convincing evidence of it night before last.”
“Oh!” said the girl in exasperation. “You would say that!”
“It’s true, isn’t it?”
“Suppose it is! I don’t have to learn the same lesson twice.”
“Well, apparently once wasn’t enough,” observed the other amiably. “You walked into the same danger tonight.”
“I wasn’t in any danger tonight!” Suddenly her mood changed as she recalled the circumstances of her parting with Nicholas Devine. “Dr. Carl,” she said, her voice dropping, “I’m terribly unhappy.”

===Chinese===
“我看出来了，”医生干巴巴地说。“前天晚上你可是给出了有力的证据。”
“哦！”姑娘恼火地说。“你偏要提这个！”
“这是实情，不是吗？”
“就算是！同一个教训我不需要领教两遍。”
“嗯，显然一回是不够的，”对方和气地说。“今晚你又走进了同一桩危险。”
“今晚我根本没处在危险里！”想起与尼古拉斯·德文分别的情形，她的情绪蓦地变了。“卡尔医生，”她说，声音低了下来，“我难受极了。”

===Original===
“Lord!” he exclaimed staring at her. “Pat, your moods are as changeable as my golf game! You’re as mercurial as your Devine lad! A moment ago you were snapping at me, and now I’m suddenly acceptable again.” He perceived the misery in her face. “All right, child; I’m listening.”
“He’s going away,” she said mournfully.
“Don’t you think that’s best for everybody concerned? I commend his judgment.”
“But I don’t want him to!”
“You do, Pat. You can’t continue seeing him, and his absence will make it easier for you.”
“It’ll never be easier for me, Dr. Carl.” She felt her eyes fill. “I guess I’m﻿—just a fool about him.”

===Chinese===
“老天！”他瞪着她叹道。“帕特，你的心绪变得比我的高尔夫球技还快！你跟德文那小子一样反复无常！刚才还冲我龇牙，这会儿我又忽然够格了。”他看出了她脸上的凄苦。“好了，孩子；我听着呢。”
“他要走了，”她凄然说。
“你不觉得这对所有相关的人都好吗？我赞赏他的判断。”
“可我不要他走！”
“你要的，帕特。你们不能再见面了，他一走，你日子会好过些。”
“对我来说永远不会好过，卡尔医生。”她感到眼里蓄满了泪。“我想我——就是为了他，甘心做个傻瓜。”

===Original===
“You still feel that way, after the experience you went through?”
“Yes. Yes, I do.”
“Then you *are* a fool about him, Pat. He’s not worth such devotion.”
“How do you know what he’s worth? I’m the only one to judge that.”
“I have eyes,” said the Doctor. “What happened tonight to change your attitude so suddenly? You were amenable to reason yesterday.”
“I didn’t know yesterday what I know now.”

===Chinese===
“经历了那一切之后，你还是这样想？”
“是的。是的，还这样。”
“那你就是为他做了傻瓜，帕特。他不值你这样痴心。”
“他值多少你怎么知道？够格评判的只有我一个。”
“我长着眼睛，”医生说。“今晚出了什么事，让你态度转变得这么急？昨天你还讲道理。”
“昨天我还不知道如今知道的这些。”

===Original===
“So he told a story, eh?” The Doctor watched her serious, troubled features. “Would you mind telling me, Honey? I’m interested in the defense mechanisms these psychopathic cases erect to explain their own impulses to themselves.”
“No, I won’t tell you!” snapped Pat indignantly. “Psychopathic cases! We’re all just cases to you. I’m a case and he’s another, and all you want is our symptoms!”
Doctor Horker smiled placatingly into her face. “Pat dear,” he said earnestly, “don’t you see I’d give my eyes to help you? Don’t take my flippancies too seriously, Honey; look once in a while at the intentions behind them.” He continued his earnest gaze.
The girl returned his look; her face softened. “I’m sorry,” she said contritely. “I never doubted it, Dr. Carl﻿—it’s only that I’m so﻿—so torn to pieces by all this that I get snappy and irritable.” She paused. “Of course I’ll tell you.”
“I’d like to hear it.”
“Well,” she began hesitantly, “he said he was two personalities﻿—one the character I knew, and one the character that we saw Saturday night. And the first one is﻿—well, dominant, and fights the other one. He says the other has been growing stronger; until lately he could suppress it. And he says﻿—oh, it sounds ridiculous, the way I tell it, but it’s true! I’m sure it’s true!” She leaned toward the Doctor. “Did you ever hear of anything like it? Did you, Dr. Carl?”

===Chinese===
“这么说他编了一套说辞，嗯？”医生注视着她严肃而烦乱的面容。“愿意讲给我听听吗，亲爱的？我对这类精神病案例为给自己的冲动找说法而竖起的自卫机制，很感兴趣。”
“不，我不告诉你！”帕特愤然厉声道。“精神病案例！我们在你眼里全是案例。我是一个案例，他是另一个案例，你要的只是我们的症状！”
霍克医生对着她的脸绽出抚慰的笑。“亲爱的帕特，”他诚恳地说，“你还不明白吗，为了帮你我连这双眼睛都肯挖出来？别把我的俏皮话太当真，亲爱的；偶尔也看看话背后的心意。”他的凝视依旧恳切。
姑娘回望他；脸色柔和下来。“对不起，”她悔疚地说。“我从来没有怀疑过，卡尔医生——只是我被这一切撕扯得——撕扯得太碎，才变得又冲又躁。”她顿了顿。“我当然会告诉你。”
“我洗耳恭听。”
“嗯，”她迟疑地开口，“他说他是两个人格——一个是我认识的那个他，一个是星期六晚上我们见到的那个。前一个——嗯，占着上风，跟另一个斗。他说另一个越来越强；直到不久之前，他还压得住它。他还说——哦，经我这么一讲，听着荒唐透了，可那是真的！我肯定是真的！”她朝医生倾过身去。“你听说过这种事吗？听说过吗，卡尔医生？”

===Original===
“No.” He shook his head, still watching her seriously. “Not exactly like that, Honey. Don’t you think he might possibly have lied to you, Pat? To excuse himself for the responsibility of Saturday night, for instance?”
“No, I don’t,” she said defiantly.
“Then you have an idea yourself what the trouble is? I judge you have.”
“Yes,” she said in low tones. “I have an idea.”
“What is it?”
“I think he’s possessed by a devil!” said the girl flatly.

===Chinese===
“没有。”他摇摇头，仍旧严肃地看着她。“不完全是这样，亲爱的。你就没想过，他可能对你撒了谎，帕特？比如，好开脱他对星期六晚上该负的责任？”
“不，他没有撒谎，”她倔强地说。
“那你自己对这毛病已有看法了？我猜你有。”
“是的，”她低声说。“我有个想法。”
“是什么？”
“我想他是被魔鬼附了体！”姑娘直截了当地说。

===Original===
A quizzical expression came into the Doctor’s face. “Well, of all the queer ideas that harum-scarum mind of yours has *ever* produced, that’s the queerest!” He broke into a chuckle.
“Queer, is it?” flared Pat. “I don’t think you and your mind-doctors know as much as a Swahili medicine-man with a mask!”
She leaped angrily to her feet, stamped viciously into the hall.
“Devil and all,” she repeated, “I love him!”
“Pat!” called the Doctor anxiously. “Pat! Where are you going, child?”
“Where do devils live?” Her voice floated tauntingly back from the front door. “Hell, of course!”

===Chinese===
医生脸上浮起一种忍俊不禁的表情。“好嘛，你那颗冒失脑袋*历来*冒出的古怪念头里，数这个最古怪！”他噗嗤笑出了声。
“古怪，是吗？”帕特火了。“我看你们这些精神科大夫，还比不上一个戴面具的斯瓦希里巫医懂得多！”
她愤然一跃而起，狠狠跺着脚走进门厅。
“连魔鬼一起，”她重复道，“我爱他！”
“帕特！”医生焦急地喊。“帕特！你要去哪儿，孩子？”
“魔鬼住在哪儿？”她的声音从前门带着嘲弄飘回来。“地狱，当然！”

## III Psychiatrics of Genius / 三 天才的精神病学
===Original===
“How do you charge﻿—by the hour?” asked Pat, as Doctor Horker returned from the hall. The sound of her mother’s departing footsteps pattered on the porch.
“Of course, Young One; like a plumber.”
“Then your rates per minute must be colossal! The only time you ever see Mother is a moment or so between bridge games.”
“I add on the time I waste with you, my dear. Such as now, waiting to look over that odd swain of yours. Didn’t you say he’d be over this evening?”
“Yes, but it’s not worth your rates to have him psychoanalyzed. I can do as well myself.”
“All right, Pat. I’ll give you a sample analysis free,” chuckled the Doctor, distributing his bulk comfortably on the davenport.

===Chinese===
“你是怎么收费的——按钟点吗？”医生从门厅回来时，帕特问道。母亲离去的脚步声还在门廊上嗒嗒作响。
“当然，小家伙；跟管子工一个样。”
“那你每分钟的收费准是天文数字！你见母亲的唯一时机，就是桥牌局间那一小会儿。”
“跟你磨掉的工夫我都算添头加进去了，亲爱的。比如眼下，等着把你那位古怪情郎瞧个究竟。你不是说他今晚要来吗？”
“是说了，可要给他做精神分析，还不值你那个价。我自己来也不差。”
“行啊，帕特。我免费送你一份分析样本，”医生轻笑着，把他那副庞大的身躯舒舒服服摊开在长沙发上。

===Original===
“I don’t like free trials,” she retorted. “I sent for a beauty-culture book once, on free trial. I was twelve years only, and returned it in seven days, but I’m still getting sales letters in the mails. I must be on every sucker list in the country.”
“So that’s the secret of your charm.”
“What is?”
“You must have read the book, I mean. If you remember the title, I might try it myself. Think it’d help?”
“Dr. Carl,” laughed the girl, “you don’t need a book on beauty culture﻿—you need one on bridge! It’s that atrocious game you play that’s bothering Mother.”
“Indeed? I shouldn’t be surprised if you were right; I’ve suspected that.”

===Chinese===
“我不喜欢免费试用，”她顶回去。“有一回我函购一本美容书，免费试用那种。我才十二岁，七天就给退了，可推销信到现在还源源不断寄来。全国每一张冤大头名单上，想必都有我。”
“原来这就是你魅力的秘诀。”
“什么秘诀？”
“我是说，那本书你肯定读了。你要还记得书名，我兴许也该试试。你觉得管用吗？”
“卡尔医生，”姑娘笑道，“你用不着美容书——你该要一本桥牌书！惹母亲心烦的，是你打的那一手臭牌。”
“是吗？你说得对我也不会吃惊；我早有疑心。”

===Original===
“Save your surprise for when I’m wrong, Doc. You’ll suffer much less from shock.”
“Confident little brat! You’re apt to get that knocked out of you some day, though I hope you never do.”
“I can take it,” grinned Pat.
“No doubt you can, but you’re an adept at handing it out. Where’s this chap of yours?”
“He’ll be along. No one’s ever stood me up on a date yet.”
“I can understand that, you imp! Is that the famous Nick?” he queried as a car purred to a stop beyond the windows.

===Chinese===
“把你的惊讶留着等我说错的时候再用吧，医生。省得受那么大刺激。”
“自信的小丫头！这份底气早晚有一天会被人敲掉——不过我希望永远不会有那一天。”
“我经得起，”帕特咧嘴一笑。
“这我毫不怀疑，不过你发放起打击来也是把好手。你那位郎君呢？”
“就来了。还没有人约会放过我鸽子呢。”
“这我能理解，你这小捣蛋！”一辆汽车在窗外低吼着停稳时，他问道。“那就是大名鼎鼎的尼克？”

===Original===
“No one else!” said the girl, glancing out. “The Big Thrill in person.”
She darted to the door. Horker turned casually to watch her as she opened it, surveying Nicholas Devine with professional nonchalance. He entered, tall, slender, with his thin sensitive features sharply outlined in the light of the hall. He cast a quick glance toward the Doctor; the latter noted the curious amber-green eyes of the lad, set wide in the lean face, deep, speculative, the eyes of a dreamer.
“Evening, Nick,” Pat was bubbling. The newcomer gave her a hasty smile, with another glance at the Doctor. “Don’t mind Dr. Carl,” she continued. “Aren’t you going to kiss me? It irks the medico, and I never miss a chance.”
Nicholas flushed in embarrassment; he gestured hesitantly, then placed a hasty peck of a kiss on the girl’s forehead. He reddened again at the Doctor’s rumble of “Young imp of Satan!”
“Not very good,” said Pat reflectively, obviously enjoying the situation. “I’ve known you to do better.” She pulled him toward the arch of the living room. “Come meet Dr. Horker. Dr. Carl, this is the aforesaid Nicholas Devine.”
“Dr. Horker,” repeated the lad, smiling diffidently. “You’re the psychiatrist and brain specialist, aren’t you, Sir?”

===Chinese===
“还能有谁！”姑娘朝外瞥了一眼说。“‘大刺激’本尊驾到。”
她一阵风似的奔向门口。她开门时，霍克随意转过身看着，以职业性的漠然打量尼古拉斯·德文。他走进来，高挑清瘦，敏感的面容在门厅灯光下轮廓分明。他朝医生迅捷地瞥了一眼；后者则留意到小伙子那双奇特的琥珀绿眼睛，在清癯的脸上升得很开，深邃、沉思，是梦想家的眼睛。
“晚上好，尼克，”帕特笑靥如花。来客匆匆朝她一笑，又瞟了医生一眼。“别在意卡尔医生，”她接着说。“你不打算吻我吗？这能让那位大夫不痛快，这机会我从不放过。”
尼古拉斯窘得脸红；他迟疑地比划了一下，然后匆匆在姑娘额头上啄了一吻。医生瓮声瓮气来了句“撒旦的小鬼头！”，他又红了脸。
“不怎么样，”帕特若有所思地说，分明在享受这个场面。“你有过表现更好的时候。”她把他往客厅的拱门那边拉。“来见见霍克医生。卡尔医生，这位就是前面说到的尼古拉斯·德文。”
“霍克医生，”小伙子重复道，腼腆地笑着。“您是精神病专家兼脑科专家，对吗，先生？”

===Original===
“So my patients believe,” rumbled the massive Doctor, rising at the introduction, and grasping the youth’s hand. “And you’re the genius Patricia has been raving about. I’m glad to have the chance of looking you over.”
Nick gave the girl a harassed glance, shifting uncomfortably, and patently at a loss for a reply. She grinned mischievously.
“Sit down, both of you,” she suggested helpfully. She seized his hat from the reluctant hands of Nick, sailing it carelessly to a chair.
“So!” boomed the Doctor, lowering his great bulk again to the davenport. He eyed the youth sitting nervously before him. “Devine, did you say?”
“Yes, sir.”
“I knew a Devine once. Colleague of mine.”

===Chinese===
“我的病人是这么信的，”魁梧的医生瓮声说道，在引见时起身，握住年轻人的手。“你就是帕特里夏赞不绝口的那位天才。很高兴有机会把你瞧个仔细。”
尼克苦恼地看了姑娘一眼，局促地挪动身子，明摆着不知如何作答。她淘气地咧嘴一笑。
“你们俩都坐，”她热心地建议。她从尼克不情愿的手里夺过帽子，随手一甩，让它飞到一把椅子上。
“哦！”医生瓮声说道，重新把他那庞大的身躯放低到长沙发上。他打量着局促坐在面前的小伙子。“德文，你是说？”
“是的，先生。”
“我从前认识一个姓德文的。我的同行。”

===Original===
“A doctor? My father was a doctor.”
“Dr. Stuart Devine?”
“Yes, sir.” He paused. “Did you say you knew him, Dr. Horker?”
“Slightly,” rumbled the other. “Only slightly.”
“I don’t remember him at all, of course, I was very young when he﻿—and my mother too﻿—died.”
“You must have been. Patricia claims you write.”

===Chinese===
“是医生吗？我父亲就是医生。”
“斯图尔特·德文医生？”
“是的，先生。”他顿了顿。“您刚才说您认识他，霍克医生？”
“点头之交，”对方瓮声说。“仅仅点头之交。”
“我对他毫无印象，当然，他去世时我还很小——我母亲也一样，先后走了。”
“想必是。帕特里夏说你写东西。”

===Original===
“I try.”
“What sort of material?”
“Why﻿—any sort. Prose or poetry; what I feel like writing.”
“Whatever inspires you, I suppose?”
“Yes, sir.” The lad flushed again.
“Ever have anything published?”

===Chinese===
“算是试着写。”
“写些什么？”
“这个——什么都写。散文或诗；想写什么写什么。”
“想必是随灵感而写？”
“是的，先生。”小伙子又红了脸。
“发表过东西吗？”

===Original===
“Yes, sir. In *Nation’s Poetry*.”
“Never heard of it.”
“It has a large circulation,” said Nick apologetically.
“Humph! Well, that’s something. Whom do you like?”
“Whom do I like?” The youth’s tone was puzzled.
“What authors﻿—writers?”

===Chinese===
“发表过，先生。在《国民诗歌》上。”
“没听说过这刊物。”
“它发行量很大，”尼克带着歉意说。
“哼！好吧，总算有所成就。你喜欢谁？”
“我喜欢谁？”小伙子的语气颇为困惑。
“哪些作家——写东西的？”

===Original===
“Oh.” He cast another uncomfortable glance at Pat. “Why﻿—I like Baudelaire, and Poe, and Swinburne, and Villon, and﻿—”
“Decadents, all of them!” sniffed the Doctor. “What prose writers?”
“Well﻿—” He hesitated﻿—“Poe again, and Stern, and Rabelais﻿—”
“Rabelais!” Horker’s voice boomed. “Well! Your taste can’t be as bad as I thought, then. There’s one we agree on, anyway. And I notice you name no moderns, which is another good point.”
“I haven’t read many moderns, sir.”
“That’s in your favor.”

===Chinese===
“哦。”他又不安地瞥了帕特一眼。“这个——我喜欢波德莱尔、爱伦·坡、斯温伯恩、维庸，还有——”
“颓废派，一个个都是！”医生嗤之以鼻。“散文家呢？”
“嗯——”他犹豫着——“还是爱伦·坡，还有斯特恩，拉伯雷——”
“拉伯雷！”霍克的声音轰然作响。“好嘛！这么说来，你的趣味不至于像我想的那么糟。至少这一位咱们意见一致。我还注意到你没提任何现代人，这又是一个优点。”
“现代人的东西我读得不多，先生。”
“这对你有利。”

===Original===
“Cut it!” put in Pat with assumed sharpness. “You’ve taken enough whacks at my generation for one day.”
“I’m glad to find one of your generation who agrees with me,” chuckled the Doctor. “At least to the extent of not reading its works.”
“I’ll teach him,” grinned Pat. “I’ll have him writing vess libre, and maybe even dadaism, in a week.”
“Maybe it won’t be much loss,” grunted Horker. “I haven’t seen any of his work yet.”
“We’ll bring some around sooner or later. We will, won’t we, Nick?”
“Of course, if you want to. But﻿—”

===Chinese===
“打住！”帕特插嘴，故意做出凌厉的口气。“一天之内，你对我这一代的敲打也够多了。”
“很高兴发现你们一代里还有个跟我一致的，”医生轻笑道。“至少在不读同代人作品这一点上。”
“我会调教他的，”帕特咧嘴笑。“不出一个星期，我就能让他写起自由诗来，没准还能写达达主义。”
“也许损失不大，”霍克哼了一声。“他的作品我还一篇没见过呢。”
“我们早晚会把一些带过来的。会的，对不对，尼克？”
“当然，只要你想。不过——”

===Original===
“He’s going to say something modest,” interrupted the girl. “He’s in the retiring mood now, but he’s apt to change any moment, and snap your surly head off.”
“Humph! I’d like to see it.”
“So’d I,” retorted Pat. “You’ve had it coming all day; maybe I’ll do it myself.”
“You have, my dear, innumerable times. But I’m like the Hydra, except that I grow only one head to replace the one you snap off.” He turned again to Nicholas. “Do you work?”
“Yes, sir. At my writing.”
“I mean how do you live?”

===Chinese===
“他又要谦虚了，”姑娘打断道。“他这会儿正处在谦退的情绪里，可随时可能变卦，把你那颗乖戾的脑袋咬下来。”
“哼！我倒想见识见识。”
“我也想，”帕特回敬道。“你这脑袋招这一天了；没准我自己动手。”
“你干这事儿，亲爱的，已经数不清多少回了。不过我像那九头蛇，只是你咬掉一颗，我重长的也只有一颗。”他又转向尼古拉斯。“你工作吗？”
“是的，先生。写作就是我的工作。”
“我是问你靠什么生活？”

===Original===
“Why,” said the youth, reddening again in embarrassment, “my parents﻿—”
“Listen!” said Pat. “That’s enough of Dr. Carl’s cross examination. You’d think he was a Victorian father who had just been approached for his daughter’s hand. We haven’t whispered any news of an engagement to you, have we, Doc?”
“No, but I’m acting﻿—”
“Sure. *In loco parentis.* We know that.”
“You’re incorrigible, Pat! I wash my hands of you. Run along, if you’re going out.”
“You’ll be telling me never to darken my own door again in the next breath!” She stretched forth a diminutive foot at the extremity of a superlatively attractive ankle, caught Nick’s hat on her toe, and kicked it expertly to his lap. “Come on, Nick. There’s a moon.”

===Chinese===
“这个，”年轻人说着，又窘得红了脸，“我父母——”
“听着！”帕特说。“卡尔医生这场盘问到此为止。人家还当他是位维多利亚时代的父亲，刚被人上门求亲呢。我们可没跟你咬过耳朵说什么订婚的消息，对吧，医生？”
“是没有，可我在以——”
“行了。*代理家长*嘛。我们知道。”
“你真是无可救药，帕特！我不管你了。要出去就快去吧。”
“下一句你就该说我再也别踏进自家门了！”她伸出一只小巧的脚——脚踝美得无可挑剔——用脚趾勾住尼克的帽子，利落地一踢，送到他腿上。“走吧，尼克。今晚有月亮。”

===Original===
“There is not!” objected the Doctor huffily. “It rises at four, as you ought to know. You didn’t see it last night, did you?”
“I didn’t notice,” said the girl. “Come on, Nick, and we’ll watch it rise tonight. We’ll check up on the Doctor’s astronomy, or is it chronology?”
“You do and I’ll know it! I can hear you come home, you imp!”
“Nice neighbor,” observed Pat airily, as she stepped to the door. “I’ll bet you peek out of the window, too.”
She ignored the Doctor’s irritated rumble as she passed into the hall, where Nick, after a diffident murmur of farewell to Horker, followed. She caught up a light cape, which he draped about her shoulders.
“Nick,” she said, “suppose you run out to the car and wait. I think I’ve stepped too hard on Dr. Carl’s corns, and I want to give him a little cheering up. Will you?”

===Chinese===
“才没有！”医生恼火地反驳。“月亮凌晨四点才升，这你该知道。昨晚你们也没见着它，对吧？”
“我没留神，”姑娘说。“走吧，尼克，今晚我们看它升起来。咱们来验证一下医生的天文学——还是该叫年代学？”
“你们敢去我就知道！你几点回家的我听得见，小捣蛋！”
“好邻居，”帕特一边朝门口走，一边轻飘飘地说。“我还打赌你会从窗户偷看呢。”
她没理会医生恼火的咕哝，径自走进门厅，尼克向霍克含糊道了声再见，跟了上去。她拿起一件轻薄的披肩，由他披上肩头。
“尼克，”她说，“你先跑出去到车里等等好不好。我想我把卡尔医生的鸡眼踩得太狠了，想去给他顺顺毛。行吗？”

===Original===
“Of course, Pat.”
She darted back into the living room, perching on the arm of the davenport beside the Doctor.
“Well?” she said, running her hand through his grizzled hair. “What’s the verdict?”
“Seems like a nice kid,” grumbled Horker reluctantly. “Nice enough, but introverted, repressed, and I shouldn’t be surprised to find him antisocial. Doesn’t adjust easily to his environment; takes refuge in a dream world of his own.”
“That’s what he accuses me of doing,” grinned Pat. “That all you’ve got against him?”
“That’s all, but where’s that streak of mastery you mentioned? You lead him around on a leash!”

===Chinese===
“当然，帕特。”
她又一阵风似地折回客厅，在医生身旁的长沙发扶手上坐下。
“怎么样？”她说着，手指梳过他斑白的头发。“判决如何？”
“像个不错的孩子，”霍克不情愿地嘟囔。“够好，但内向、压抑，而且要我发现他反社会，我也不会吃惊。不容易适应环境；躲进一个自造的梦幻世界里避难。”
“这正是他指控我的罪状，”帕特咧嘴一笑。“你对他的不满就这些？”
“就这些。可你提过的那股主宰劲儿哪儿去了？你可是牵着他用皮带遛的！”

===Original===
“It didn’t show up tonight. That’s the thrill﻿—the unexpectedness of it.”
“Bah! You must’ve dreamed it. There’s no more aggressiveness in that lad than in KoKo, your canary.”
“Don’t you believe it, Dr. Carl! The trouble is that he’s a genius, and that’s where your psychology falls flat.”
“Genius,” said the Doctor oracularly, “is a sublimation of qualities﻿—”
“I’ll tell you tomorrow how sublime the qualities are,” called Pat as she skipped out of the door.

===Chinese===
“今晚它没露头。妙就妙在这儿——那份出其不意。”
“呸！准是你做梦梦见的。那小子的进取心，不比你家金丝雀科科多半分。”
“你可别信这个，卡尔医生！问题在于他是个天才，而这一点恰恰让你的心理学栽了跟头。”
“天才嘛，”医生一副神谕的口气说，“是种种品质的升华——”
“品质有多崇高，我明天再告诉你，”帕特蹦出门去时喊道。

## I Pure Horror / 一 纯粹的恐怖
===Original===
“That isn’t what I mean,” said Nicholas Devine, turning his eyes on his companion. “I mean pure horror in the sense of horror detached from experience, apart from reality. Not just a formless fear, which implies either fear of something that *might* happen, or fear of unknown dangers. Do you see what I mean?”
“Of course,” said Pat, letting her eyes wander over the black expanse of night-dark Lake Michigan. “Certainly I see what you mean but I don’t quite understand how you’d do it. It sounds﻿—well, difficult.”
She gazed at his lean profile, clear-cut against the distant light. He had turned, staring thoughtfully over the lake, idly fingering the levers on the steering wheel before him. The girl wondered a little at her feeling of contentment; she, Patricia Lane, satisfied to spend an evening in nothing more exciting than conversation! And they must have parked here a full two hours now. There was something about Nick﻿—she didn’t understand exactly what; sensitivity, charm, personality. Those were meaningless cliches, handles to hold the unexplainable nuances of character.
“It *is* difficult,” resumed Nick. “Baudelaire tried it, Poe tried it. And in painting, Hogarth, Goya, Dore. Poe came closest, I think; he caught the essence of horror in an occasional poem or story. Don’t you think so?”
“I don’t know,” said Pat. “I’ve forgotten most of my Poe.”
“Remember that story of his﻿—‘The Black Cat’?”

===Chinese===
“我不是那个意思，”尼古拉斯·德文说着，把目光转向同伴。“我指的是纯粹意义上的恐怖，一种脱离经验、远离现实的恐怖。不只是无形的恐惧——那无非是害怕*可能*发生的事，或是害怕未知的危险。你明白我的意思吗？”
“当然，”帕特说着，任目光游过夜色深沉、黑茫茫一片的密歇根湖。“你的意思我自然明白，只是不太懂你要怎么做到。这听上去——嗯，很难。”
她凝视他清瘦的侧影，在远处灯火的映衬下轮廓分明。他已转过身，若有所思地望着湖面，手指漫不经心地拨弄面前的方向盘。姑娘对自己这份心满意足略感意外；她，帕特里夏·莱恩，居然满足于把一个夜晚花在聊天上，再无更刺激的节目！他们在这儿泊车，只怕足足有两个钟头了。尼克身上有种东西——她说不清是什么；敏感、魅力、个性。这些全是没意义的陈词滥调，是些把手，用来把握性格中那些无从解释的幽微。
“确实*很*难，”尼克接着说。“波德莱尔试过，爱伦·坡试过。绘画里，贺加斯、戈雅、多雷也试过。我认为爱伦·坡最接近；他在偶一为之的诗或小说里捉住了恐怖的精髓。你不这么认为吗？”
“我说不好，”帕特说。“爱伦·坡的东西我大多忘了。”
“记得他那篇——《黑猫》吗？”

===Original===
“Dimly. The man murdered his wife.”
“Yes. That isn’t the part I mean. I mean the cat itself﻿—the second cat. You know a cat, used rightly, can be a symbol of horror.”
“Indeed yes!” The girl shuddered. “I don’t like the treacherous beasts!”
“And this cat of Poe’s,” continued Nick, warming to his subject. “Just think of it﻿—in the first place, it’s black; element of horror. Then, it’s gigantic, unnaturally, abnormally large. And then it’s not all black﻿—that would be inartistically perfect﻿—but has a formless white mark on its breast, a mark that little by little assumes a fantastic form﻿—do you remember what?”
“No.”
“The form of a gallows!”

===Chinese===
“模模糊糊。那男人杀了自己的妻子。”
“对。可我指的不是那部分。我指的是猫本身——第二只猫。你知道，猫要是用对了地方，能成为恐怖的象征。”
“可不是嘛！”姑娘打了个寒噤。“这种阴险的畜生我不喜欢！”
“再说爱伦·坡这只猫，”尼克谈兴渐浓地往下说。“你想啊——首先，它是黑的；恐怖要素。其次，它大得骇人，不自然地、反常地硕大。然后它还不是纯黑的——纯黑就完美得不合艺术了——它胸口有块形状不定的白斑，那斑一点一点显出一个诡异的形状——你记得是什么吗？”
“不记得。”
“绞刑架的形状！”

===Original===
“Oh!” said the girl. “Ugh!”
“And then﻿—climax of genius﻿—the eyes! Blind in one eye, the other a baleful yellow orb! Do you feel it? A black cat, an enormous black cat marked with a gallows, and lacking one eye, to make the other even more terrible! Literary tricks, of course, but they work, and *that’s* genius! Isn’t it?”
“Genius! Yes, if you call it that. The perverse genius of the Devil!”
“That’s what I want to write﻿—what I will write some day.” He watched the play of lights on the restless surface of the waters. “Pure horror, the epitome of the horrible. It could be written, but it hasn’t been yet; not even by Poe.”
“That little analysis of yours was bad enough, Nick! Why should you want to improve on his treatment of the theme?”
“Because I like to write, and because I’m interested in the horrible. Two good reasons.”

===Chinese===
“哦！”姑娘叫了一声。“呃！”
“然后——天才的顶点——那双眼睛！一只眼是瞎的，另一只是颗凶光四射的黄球！你感觉到了吗？一只黑猫，一只硕大无朋、烙着绞刑架印记的黑猫，还瞎了一只眼，好让另一只愈发可怖！当然是文学手法，可它们就是管用，这*就是*天才！不是吗？”
“天才！好吧，随你怎么叫它。魔鬼的那种乖邪天才！”
“那正是我要写的——总有一天我会写出来。”他望着光亮在不平静的水面上跳动。“纯粹的恐怖，恐怖的极致。它是写得出来的，只是至今还没人写出来过；连爱伦·坡也没写出来过。”
“你那套小小的分析已经够吓人了，尼克！你为什么还想在他的主题处理上更上一层楼？”
“因为我喜欢写，也因为我痴迷于可怖之物。两条过硬的理由。”

===Original===
“Two excuses, you mean. Of course, even if you’d succeed, you couldn’t force anyone to read it.”
“If I succeed, there’d be no need to force people. Success would mean that the thing would be great literature, and even today, in these times, there are still people to read that. And besides﻿—” He paused.
“Besides what?”
“Everybody’s interested in the horrible. Even you are, whether or not you deny it.”
“I certainly do deny it!”
“But you are, Pat. It’s natural to be.”

===Chinese===
“你是说两条托词吧。再说，就算你写成了，你也强迫不了任何人去读它。”
“我要是写成了，就用不着强迫谁。成功就意味着那东西会是伟大的文学，而即便是今天，在这个时代，读这种东西的人总还是有的。再说——”他停住了。
“再说什么？”
“人人都对可怖之物感兴趣。连你也是，不管你承认不承认。”
“我当然不承认！”
“可你确实如此，帕特。这是天性。”

===Original===
“It isn’t!”
“Then what is?”
“Interest in people, and life, and gay times, and pretty things, and﻿—and one’s self and one’s own feelings. And the feelings of the people one loves.”
“Yes. It comes to exactly the point I’ve been stressing. People are sordid, life is hopeless, gay times are stupid, beauty is sensual, one’s own feelings are selfish. And love is carnal. That’s the array of horrors that holds your interest!”
The girl laughed in exasperation. “Nick, you could out-argue your namesake, the Devil himself! Do you really believe that indictment of the normal viewpoint?”
“I do﻿—often!”

===Chinese===
“才不是！”
“那什么才是？”
“对人、对生活、对快活日子、对漂亮东西的兴趣，还有——还有对自己、对自己感受的兴趣。对自己所爱之人的感受的兴趣。”
“不错。这恰恰落在我一直强调的那个点上。人是卑污的，生活无望，快活日子愚蠢，美是肉感的，自己的感受自私自利。而爱是肉欲的。这一整排恐怖，正是攥住你兴趣的东西！”
姑娘又好气又好笑。“尼克，你辩起来能压过你的同名者，魔鬼他老人家！那份对正常人生观的诉状，你真信？”
“真信——经常信！”

===Original===
“Now?”
“Now,” he said, turning his gaze on Pat, “I have no feeling of it at all. Now, right now, I don’t believe it.”
“Why not?” she queried, smiling ingenuously at him.
“You, obviously.”
“Gracious! I had no idea my logic was as convincing as that.”
“Your logic isn’t. The rest of you is.”

===Chinese===
“现在呢？”
“现在，”他把目光转向帕特，“我全无那种感觉。现在，就在此刻，我不信它。”
“为什么不信？”她问，朝他坦然一笑。
“明摆着，因为你。”
“哎呀！没想到我的逻辑竟这么有说服力。”
“你的逻辑不算。你其余的部分算。”

===Original===
“That sounds like a compliment,” observed Pat. “If it is,” she continued in a bantering tone, “it’s the only one I can recall obtaining from you.”
“That’s because I seldom call attention to the obvious.”
“And that’s another,” laughed the girl. “I’ll have to mark this date in red on my calendar. It’s entirely unique in our﻿—let’s see﻿—nearly a month’s acquaintance.”
“Is it really so short a time? I know you so well that it must have taken years. Every detail!” He closed his eyes. “Hair like black silk, and oddly dark blue eyes﻿—if I were writing a poem at the moment, I’d call them violet. Tiny lips, the sort the Elizabethan called bee-stung. Straight nose, and a figure that is a sort of vest-pocket copy of Diana. Right?” He opened his eyes.
“Nice, but exaggerated. And even if you were correct, that isn’t Pat Lane, the real Pat Lane. A camera could do better on a tenth of a second’s acquaintance!”
“Check!” He closed his eyes again. “Personality, piquant. Character, loyal, naturally happy, intelligent, but not serious. An intellectual butterfly; a dilettante. Poised, cool, self-possessed, yet inherently affectionate. A being untouched by reality, as yet, living in Chicago and in a make-believe world at the same time.” He paused, “How old are you, Pat?”

===Chinese===
“听着像句恭维，”帕特说。“要是真是，”她用打趣的口气接着说，“那是我记得从你这儿挣到的唯一一句。”
“那是因为我很少对显而易见的东西多加留意。”
“这又是一句，”姑娘笑起来。“我得在日历上把今天圈红。在我们——让我算算——将近一个月的交情里，这可是独一份。”
“真有这么短吗？我对你了如指掌，想必是花了多年的。每一处细节！”他闭上眼。“头发如黑绸，一双出奇深的蓝眼睛——此刻若是在写诗，我会称它们为紫罗兰色。小巧的唇，正是伊丽莎白时代人称作‘蜂蜇唇’的那种。挺直的鼻梁，一副身子，好比狄安娜的袖珍版。对不对？”他睁开眼。
“不赖，就是夸张了。就算你说的都对，那也不是帕特·莱恩，不是真正的帕特·莱恩。一架照相机认识人十分之一秒，都比你强！”
“认罚！”他又闭上眼。“个性，俏皮。品性，忠实，天性快乐，聪颖，但不严肃。一只智识上的蝴蝶；一个浅尝辄止的玩家。端庄、冷静、自持，骨子里却重情。一个至今未被现实沾染的生灵，同时活在芝加哥和一个虚幻世界里。”他顿了顿。“你多大了，帕特？”

===Original===
“Twenty-two. Why?”
“I wondered how long one could manage to stay in the world of make-believe. I’m twenty-six, and I’m long exiled.”
“I don’t think you know what you mean by a make-believe world. I’m sure I don’t.”
“Of course you don’t. You can’t know and still remain there. It’s like being happy; once you realize it, it’s no longer perfect.”
“Then don’t explain!”
“Wouldn’t make any difference if I did, Pat. It’s a queer world, like the Sardoodledom of Sardou and the afternoon-tea school of playwrights. All stage-settings and pretense, but it looks real while you’re watching, especially if you’re one of the characters.”

===Chinese===
“二十二。问这个干什么？”
“我纳闷，一个人能在虚幻世界里赖到多久。我二十六，早被放逐多年了。”
“我看你并不清楚你说的虚幻世界是什么意思。我敢肯定我不清楚。”
“你当然不清楚。人不可能既知道，又留在里面。这跟快乐一样；一旦你意识到了它，它就不再完美。”
“那就别解释！”
“我解释了也白搭，帕特。那是个古怪的世界，就像萨尔杜的造作天地和下午茶派的剧作家笔下那样。全是布景和做戏，可你看戏的时候它显得真极了，尤其是你自己也是戏中人之一的时候。”

===Original===
The girl laughed. “You’re a deliciously solemn sort, Nick. How would you like to hear my analysis of you?”
“I wouldn’t!”
“You inflicted yours on me, and I’m entitled to revenge. And so﻿—you’re intelligent, lazy, dreamy, and with a fine perception of artistic values. You’re very alert to impressions of the senses﻿—I mean you’re sensuous without being sensual. You’re delightfully serious without being somber, except sometimes. Sometimes I feel a hint, just a thrilling hint, in your character, of something dangerously darker﻿—”
“Don’t!” said Nick sharply.
Pat shot him a quick glance. “And you’re frightened to death of falling in love,” she concluded imperturbably.
“Oh! Do you think so?”

===Chinese===
姑娘笑了。“你这人一本正经得可爱，尼克。想不想听听我对你的分析？”
“不想！”
“你把你的分析强加给了我，我有权报复。那么——你聪明、懒惰、爱做梦，对艺术价值有精妙的鉴赏力。你对感官印象极其敏锐——我是说，你耽于感官而不流于肉欲。你认真得讨人喜欢，又不阴郁，只是偶尔例外。有时候我在你的性格里觉出一丝苗头，仅仅一丝叫人心悸的苗头，藏着一层危险得更深的东西——”
“别说！”尼克厉声道。
帕特飞快地瞥他一眼。“还有，你对坠入爱河怕得要死，”她不动声色地下结论。
“哦！你这么想？”

===Original===
“I do.”
“Then you’re wrong! I can’t be afraid of it, since I’ve known for the better part of a month that I’ve been in love.”
“With me,” said the girl.
“Yes, with you!”
“Well!” said Pat. “It never before took me a month to extract that admission from a man. Is twenty-two getting old?”
“You’re a tantalizing imp!”

===Chinese===
“我这么想。”
“那你可错了！我不可能怕它，因为差不多一个月来，我一直清楚自己恋爱了。”
“跟我，”姑娘说。
“对，跟你！”
“好嘛！”帕特说。“从前从没哪个男人要我花整整一个月才肯吐露这句自白。是不是二十二岁就老了？”
“你真是个磨人的小妖精！”

===Original===
“And so?” She pursed her lips, assuming an air of disappointment. “What am I to do about it﻿—scream for help? You haven’t given me anything to scream about.”
The kiss, Pat admitted to herself, was quite satisfactory. She yielded herself to the pleasure of it; it was decidedly the best kiss she had, in her somewhat limited experience, encountered. She pushed herself away finally, with a little gasp, gazing bright-eyed at her companion. He was staring down at her with serious eyes; there was a tense twist to his mouth, and a curiously unexpected attitude of unhappiness.
“Nick!” she murmured. “Was it as bad as all that?”
“Bad! Pat, does it mean you﻿—care for me? A little, anyway?”
“A little,” she admitted. “Maybe more. Is that what makes you look so forlorn?”
He drew her closer to him. “How could I look forlorn, Honey, when something like this has happened to me? That was just my way of looking happy.”

===Chinese===
“那又如何？”她嘟起嘴，摆出一副失望的架势。“我该拿它怎么办——尖声呼救吗？你可没给出什么值得我尖叫的东西。”
这个吻，帕特暗自承认，相当令人满意。她任自己沉溺于其中的愉悦；在她不算丰富的阅历里，这分明是她遭遇过的最佳一吻。最终她轻轻喘息着推开自己，亮晶晶的眼睛望着同伴。他正低头凝视她，眼神严肃；嘴角绷出一道紧张的扭曲，还带着一种出人意料的颓丧。
“尼克！”她低声道。“有那么糟吗？”
“糟！帕特，这是不是说明你——心里有我？至少有一点点？”
“有一点，”她承认。“也许不止。你就是为这个才显得这么凄惶？”
他把她拉近了些。“这样的事落在我身上，亲爱的，我怎么会显得凄惶？那只不过是我表达高兴的样子。”

===Original===
She nestled as closely as the steering wheel permitted, drawing his arm about her shoulders. “I hope you mean that, Nick.”
“Then *you* mean it? You really do?”
“I really do.”
“I’m glad,” he said huskily. The girl thought she detected a strange dubious note in his voice. She glanced at his face; his eyes were gazing into the dim remoteness of the night horizon.
“Nick,” she said, “why were you so﻿—well, so reluctant about admitting this? You must have known I﻿—like you. I showed you that deliberately in so many ways.”
“I﻿—I wasn’t quite sure.”

===Chinese===
她在方向盘许可的范围内尽量偎紧他，拉过他的手臂环住自己的肩。“我希望你说的是真心话，尼克。”
“那*你*是真心的？真的？”
“真的是。”
“我很高兴，”他嗓音发哑地说。姑娘觉得自己听出他声音里有一丝奇怪的迟疑。她看了看他的脸；他的双眼正望进夜幕地平线朦胧的深处。
“尼克，”她说，“你为什么这么——嗯，这么不情愿承认这件事？你一定知道我——喜欢你。我变着法子、存心让你看明白过多少回。”
“我——我不太确定。”

===Original===
“You were! That isn’t it, Nick. I had to practically browbeat you into confessing you cared for me. Why?”
He stepped on the starter; the motor ground into sudden life. The car backed into the road, turning toward Chicago, that glared like a false dawn in the southern sky.
“I hope you never find out,” he said.

===Chinese===
“你确定！不是这么回事，尼克。我几乎是连哄带吓，才逼你说出你在意我。为什么？”
他踩下启动踏板；马达轰然运转起来。车子退上大路，朝芝加哥驶去——那片城光在南边天际灼灼耀眼，宛如一场虚假的黎明。
“我希望你永远别知道，”他说。

## X Rescue from Abaddon / 十 从亚巴顿获救
===Original===
Pat slid dizzily from her perch on the table and sank heavily to a chair. The interruption of the mustached keeper of this den of contradictions struck her as extremely humorous; she giggled hysterically as her wavering gaze perceived the consternation in his sharp little face. Some forlorn shred of modesty asserted itself, and she dragged a corner of the red-checked tablecloth across her knees.
“Get out!” said Nicholas Devine in that voice of rasping metal. “Get out!” he repeated in unchanging tones.
The other made no move to leave. “Yeah?” he said. “Listen, Bud﻿—this place is respectable, see? You want to pull something like this, you go upstairs, see? And pay for your room.”
“Get out!” There was no variation in the voice.
“*You* get out! The both of you, see?”
Nicholas Devine stepped slowly toward him; his back, as he advanced upon the bartender, was toward Pat, yet through the haze of intoxication, she had an impression of evil red eyes in a chill, impassive face. “Get out!”

===Chinese===
帕特晕乎乎地从桌上一滑，重重跌坐进椅子。这个留着小胡子的、掌管这处藏污纳垢之所的看守跑来打断，在她看来滑稽透顶；她迷离的目光瞥见他精瘦小脸上那副惊惶，便歇斯底里地咯咯直笑。一缕残存的羞耻心冒出头来，她拽过红格桌布的一角，盖在膝上。
“滚出去！”尼古拉斯·德文用那种锉金属般的嗓音说道。“滚出去！”他又原调原腔地重复了一遍。
对方没有挪窝的意思。“是吗？”他说。“听着，老兄——这地方是体面铺子，懂吗？想玩这套，就上楼去开个房，明白？房钱照付。”
“滚出去！”嗓音没有一丝起伏。
“*你们*滚出去！你们俩，懂吗？”
尼古拉斯·德文缓缓朝他逼近；他扑向酒吧老板时背对着帕特，然而透过醺醺酒雾，她还是捕捉到一幅印象：一张冰冷漠然的脸，一双邪恶的红眼睛。“滚出去！”

===Original===
The other had no stomach for such an adversary. He backed out of the door, closing it as he vanished. His voice floated in from the hall.
“I’m telling you!” he called. “Clear out!”
Nicholas Devine turned back toward the girl. He surveyed her sitting in her chair; she had dropped her chin to her hand to steady the whirling of her head.
“We’ll go,” he said. “Come on.”
“I just want to sit here,” she said. “Just let me sit here. I’m tired.”
“Come on,” he repeated.

===Chinese===
对手如此强横，老板没了应战的胆气。他倒退着出了门，人影消失时顺势带上了门。他的声音从门厅飘进来。
“我把话撂这儿！”他喊道。“赶紧滚蛋！”
尼古拉斯·德文转回身面向姑娘。他打量着坐在椅子里的她；她已把下巴撂在手上，好稳住脑袋里的天旋地转。
“我们走，”他说。“来。”
“我只想坐在这儿，”她说。“就让我坐在这儿。我累了。”
“走，”他重复道。

===Original===
“Why?” she muttered petulantly. “I’m tired.”
“I want no interruptions. We’ll go elsewhere.”
“Must dress!” she murmured dazedly, “can’t go on street without dress.”
Nicholas Devine swept her frock from its place in the corner, gathered her wrap from the chair, and flung them over his arm. He grasped her wrist, tugging her to an unsteady standing position.
“Come on,” he said.
“Dress!”

===Chinese===
“为什么？”她赌气地咕哝。“我累了。”
“我不要人打扰。我们换个地方。”
“得穿衣裳！”她迷迷瞪瞪地喃喃，“没穿衣裳，没法上街。”
尼古拉斯·德文从墙角把她的连衣裙一扫而就，又从椅上抓起她的披肩，两样一股脑甩上自己的手臂。他攥住她的手腕，把她拽成一个摇摇晃晃的立姿。
“走，”他说。
“衣裳！”

===Original===
He snatched the red checked tablecloth from its place, precipitating bottles, ashtray, and glasses into an indiscriminate pile, and threw the stained and odorous fabric across her shoulders. She gathered it about her like a toga; it hung at most points barely below her waist, but it satisfied the urge of her muddled mind for a covering of some sort.
“We’ll go through the rear,” her companion said. “Into the alley. I want no trouble with that rat in the bar﻿—yet!”
He still held Pat’s wrist; she stumbled after him as he dragged her into the darkness of the hall. They moved through it blindly to a door at the far end; Nicholas swung it open upon a dim corridor flanked by buildings on either side, with a strip of star-sprinkled sky above.
Pat’s legs were somehow incapable of their usual lithe grace; she failed to negotiate the single step, and crashed heavily to the concrete paving. The shock and the cooler air of the open steadied her momentarily; she felt no pain from her bruised knees, but a temporary rift in the fog that bound her mind. She gathered the red-checked cloth more closely about her shoulders as her companion, still clutching her wrist, jerked her violently to her feet.
They moved into the gulch of the alley, and here she found difficulty in following. Her tiny high-heeled pumps slipped at every step on the uneven cobbles of the paving, and the unsteady footing made her lurch and stumble until the dusty stretch of the alley was a writhing panorama of shadows and lighted windows and stars. Nicholas Devine turned an impatient glare on her, and here in the semidarkness, his face was again the face of the red-eyed demon. She dragged him to a halt, laughing strangely.
“There it is!” she cried, pointing at him with her free hand. He turned again, staring at her with grim features.

===Chinese===
他一把扯掉那块红格桌布，瓶子和烟灰缸、杯子哗啦啦堆作一处，随即将那块渍迹斑斑、气味刺鼻的织物罩上她的肩。她像裹托加袍似的把它拢在身上；大多数地方都垂不过腰际，可它总归满足了那颗混沌脑袋对某种遮体的渴求。
“我们从后头走，”她的同伴说。“进巷子。我还不想跟酒吧里那只耗子纠缠——暂时还不想！”
他仍攥着帕特的手腕；他拖着她钻进门厅的黑暗，她跌跌撞撞跟在后面。两人摸黑走到尽头一扇门前；尼古拉斯推开门，外面是一条两侧楼房夹峙的昏暗过道，头顶一线缀满星斗的天空。
帕特的两条腿怎么也使不出平日的轻灵；区区一级台阶她都没能下稳，重重摔在水泥地上。这一撞加上露天里更凉的空气，让她一时清醒了些；擦伤的膝盖并不觉得疼，只觉得捆住头脑的浓雾裂开了一道暂时的口子。同伴仍攥着她的手腕，狠狠把她拽起来，她把红格布往肩上拢得更紧了些。
两人走进巷子这道夹缝，在这儿她可跟不上趟了。她那双小巧的高跟鞋在坑洼不平的鹅卵石上步步打滑，脚下虚浮，她东倒西歪、踉踉跄跄，尘埃弥漫的巷子在她眼里成了一幅扭曲翻滚的全景，阴影、亮灯的窗子和星星绞成一团。尼古拉斯·德文不耐烦地瞪过来，就在这半明半暗里，他的脸又成了红眼恶魔的脸。她拽得他停下步，怪声怪气地笑起来。
“在那儿！”她喊道，用空着的那只手指着他。他回过头，面目狰狞地瞪着她。

===Original===
“What?”
“There! Your face﻿—the face of evil!” Again she laughed hysterically.
The other stepped to her side; the disturbing eyes were inches from her own. He raised his hand as she laughed, slapped her sharply, so that her head reeled. He seized her shoulders, shaking her until the checkered cloth billowed like a flag in a wind.
“Now come!” he muttered.
But the girl, laughing no longer, leaned pale and weak against a low board fence. Her limbs seemed paralyzed, and movement was quite impossible. She was conscious of neither the blow nor the shaking, but only of a devastating nausea and an all-encompassing weakness. She bent over the fence; she was violently ill.
Then the nausea had vanished, and a weariness, a strange lassitude, was all that remained. Nicholas Devine stood over her; suddenly he pressed her body to him in a convulsive embrace, so that her head dropped back, and his face loomed above her, obliterating the stars.

===Chinese===
“什么？”
“那儿！你的脸——那张邪恶的脸！”她又歇斯底里地笑起来。
对方跨到她跟前；那双慑人的眼睛离她自己的只有几寸。趁她笑着，他扬手便是一记耳光，打得她脑袋嗡嗡直转。他揪住她的双肩摇晃，摇得那块方格布像风中的旗子一样翻卷。
“这下走了！”他咕哝道。
可姑娘已笑不出来了，苍白虚弱地倚着低矮的木板围栏。她的四肢仿佛瘫了，动弹不得。那记耳光、那阵摇晃，她都浑然不觉，只觉得一阵翻天覆地的恶心和一种淹没四肢百骸的虚弱。她伏在围栏上，剧烈地呕吐起来。
随后恶心退去了，剩下的只有疲惫，一种奇异的倦怠。尼古拉斯·德文俯身立在她上方；他突然痉挛似的把她按进怀里，压得她头向后仰，他的脸在上方逼临下来，遮灭了满天星斗。

===Original===
“Ah!” he said. He seemed about to kiss her when a sound﻿—voices﻿—filtered out of somewhere in the maze of dark courts and littered yards along the alley. He released her, seized her wrist, and once more she was stumbling wretchedly behind him over the uneven surface of the cobblestones.
A numbness had come over her; consciousness burned very low as she wavered doggedly along through the darkness. She perceived dimly that they were approaching the end of the alley; the brighter glow of the street loomed before them, and a passing motor car cut momentary parallel shafts of luminescence across the opening.
Nicholas Devine slowed his pace, still clutching her wrist in a cold grip; he paused, moving cautiously toward the corner of the building. He peered around the edge of the structure, surveying the now deserted street, while Pat stood dully behind him, incapable alike of thought or voluntary movement, clutching desperately at the dirty cloth that hung about her shoulders.
Her companion finished his survey; apparently satisfied that progress was safe, he dragged her after him, turning toward the corner beyond which his car was parked. The girl staggered behind him with diminishing vigor; consciousness was very nearly at the point of disappearance, and her steps were wavering unsteadily, and doggedly slow. She dragged heavily on his arm; he gave a gesture of impatience at her weakness.
“Come on!” he growled. “We’re just going to the corner.” His voice rose slightly in pitch, still sounding harsh as rasping metals. “There still remains the ultimate evil!” he said. “There is still a depth of beauty unplumbed, a pain whose exquisite pleasure is yet to find!”
They approached the corner; abruptly Nicholas Devine drew back as two figures came unexpectedly into view from beyond it. He turned back toward the alleyway, dragging the girl in a dizzy circle. He took a few rapid steps.

===Chinese===
“啊！”他说。他眼看就要吻她，这时一个声响——人声——从巷子边那片黑暗院落和垃圾遍地的后户组成的迷宫里渗了出来。他放开她，攥住她的手腕，于是她又凄凄惶惶地跟在他身后，跌跌撞撞走过高低不平的鹅卵石。
一阵麻木攫住了她；她倔强地在黑暗中摇晃着前行，意识只剩一点将熄的微火。她隐约觉出他们正在接近巷口；街道更亮的光在前方隐隐逼来，一辆驶过的汽车掠过巷口，划下几道转瞬即逝的平行光柱。
尼古拉斯·德文放慢脚步，仍用一只冰冷的手攥着她的腕子；他停了停，悄没声地挪向楼房的墙角。他从楼缘探出头，扫视此刻已然空寂的街道；帕特呆立在他身后，思绪和自主的动作一概无能，只死死攥着挂在肩上的那块脏布。
同伴查看完毕；看来是确认了行动安全，他拖着她跟上，拐向街角——他的车就停在那后头。姑娘跟在后面，越走越没力气；意识已到了消散的边缘，脚步虚浮凌乱，却又固执地缓慢。她沉沉地坠着他的手臂；他为她这副孱弱做了个不耐烦的手势。
“快点！”他咆哮道。“不过是走到街角。”他的声调微微拔高，听来依然像锉金属般粗粝。“终极之恶还在后头！”他说。“还有一层美未及探测的深底，还有一种痛，它精妙的快感尚待发掘！”
两人走近街角；骤然间，两个身影从拐角后头意外地冒出来，尼古拉斯·德文猛地缩身。他掉头折向巷子，拖得姑娘晕头转向地兜了个圈。他疾步抢了几步。

===Original===
But Pat was through, exhausted. At his first step she stumbled and sprawled, dragging prone behind him. He released her hand and turned defiantly to face the approaching men, while the girl lying on the pavement struggled to a sitting posture with her back against the wall. She turned dull, indifferent eyes on the scene, then was roused to a somewhat higher pitch of interest by the sound of a familiar voice.
“There he is! I told you it was his car.”
Dr. Horker! She struggled for clarity of thought; she realized dimly that she ought to feel relief, happiness﻿—but all she could summon was a faint quickening of interest, or rather, a diminution of the lassitude that held her. She drew the rag of a tablecloth about her and huddled against the wall, watching. The Doctor and some strange man, burly and massive in the darkness, dashed upon them, while Nicholas Devine waited, his red-orbed face a demoniac picture of cold contempt. Then the Doctor glanced at her huddled, bedraggled figure; she saw his face aghast, incredulous, as he perceived the condition of her clothing.
“Pat! My God, girl! What’s happened? Where’ve you been?”
She found a hidden reserve somewhere within her. Her voice rose, shrill and hysterical.
“We’ve been in Hell!” she said. “You came to take me back, didn’t you? Orpheus and Eurydice!” She laughed. “Dr. Orpheus Horker!”

===Chinese===
可帕特已经撑到了头，力气用尽。他刚迈第一步，她就绊倒在地，直挺挺拖在他身后。他松开她的手，转身昂然迎向来人；躺在人行道上的姑娘挣扎着坐起，背抵着墙。她目光呆滞、漠然地望着这一幕，随后，一个熟悉的声音让她提起了一点儿兴趣。
“他在那儿！我说过这是他的车吧。”
霍克医生！她挣扎着想清醒地思考；她模模糊糊地意识到自己该感到宽慰、感到欢喜——可她能唤起的，只有一点微弱的兴趣，或者不如说，只是捆着她的那股倦怠轻了少许。她把那块破桌布往身上拢了拢，缩着靠在墙上观望。医生和一个陌生的男人——黑暗中显得魁梧壮实——朝他们猛冲过来，尼古拉斯·德文静候不动，那张红眼的面孔是一幅冷然轻蔑的恶魔画像。随后医生瞥见她缩成一团、狼狈不堪的样子；看清她衣衫的境况时，她看见他脸上写满惊骇与难以置信。
“帕特！我的天，姑娘！出什么事了？你们上哪儿去了？”
她从体内某处翻出一股潜藏的余力。她的声音拔高起来，尖利而歇斯底里。
“我们去了一趟地狱！”她说。“你是来接我回去的，对不对？俄耳甫斯和欧律狄刻！”她笑起来。“俄耳甫斯·霍克医生！”

===Original===
The Doctor flashed her another incredulous glance and a grim and very terrible expression flamed in his face. He turned toward Nicholas Devine, his hands clenching, his mouth twisting without utterance, with no sound save a half-audible snarl. Then he spoke, a low, grating phrase flung at his thickset companion.
“Bring the car,” was all he said. The man lumbered away toward the corner, and he turned again toward Nicholas Devine, who faced him impassively. Suddenly his fist shot out; he struck the youth or demon squarely between the red eyes, sending him reeling back against the building. Then the Doctor turned, bending over Pat; she felt the pressure of his arms beneath knees and shoulders. He was carrying her toward a car that drew up at the curb; he was placing her gently in the back seat. Then, without a glance at the figure still leaning against the building, he swept from the sidewalk the dark mass that was Pat’s dress and her wrap, and reentered the car beside her.
“Shall I turn him in?” asked the man in the front seat.
“We can’t afford the publicity,” said the Doctor, adding grimly, “I’ll settle with him later.”
Pat’s head lurched as the car started; she was losing consciousness, and realized it vaguely, but she retained one impression as the vehicle swung into motion. She perceived that the face of the lone figure leaning against the building, a face staring at her with horror and unbelief, was no longer the visage of the demon of the evening, but that of her own Nick.

===Chinese===
医生再度朝她投去难以置信的一瞥，一张脸腾起冷峻而极为骇人的神情。他转向尼古拉斯·德文，双手攥紧，嘴扭动着却未出声，只发出一声半可听闻的低吼。随后他开了口，把一句低沉刺耳的话甩给壮实的同伴。
“把车开过来，”他只说了这一句。那男人迈着沉重的步子朝街角去了，他重新转向尼古拉斯·德文——后者无动于衷地对着他。他的拳头猛然击出；正中那年轻人——或者恶魔——两只红眼之间，打得他踉跄倒退，撞在楼上。随后医生转过身，俯向帕特；她感到一双胳膊托进她的膝弯和肩下。他正抱着她走向一辆停靠路缘的车；正把她轻轻放进后座。随后，对那个仍倚着楼房的身影看也不看一眼，他从人行道上扫起那团黑色的东西——帕特的连衣裙和披肩——钻进车里，坐在她身旁。
“要不要把他交给警察？”前排的男人问。
“我们经不起曝光，”医生说，又冷冷补了一句，“这笔账我以后跟他算。”
车子发动，帕特的头歪了一下；她正在失去意识，也隐约知道这一点，但车驶动的一瞬，她留住了一个印象。她觉出，那个独自倚着楼房的身影，那张带着惊骇与难以置信死死望着她的脸，已不再是今晚恶魔的嘴脸，而是她自己的尼克的。

## XXXII Revelation / 三十二 启示
===Original===
“Is it over now?” queried Pat tremulously as the Doctor finally reappeared. The interminable waiting had left her even more worn, and her pallid features bore the marks of strain.
“Twenty minutes ago,” said Horker. His face too bore evidence of tension; moreover, there was a puzzled, dubious expression in his eyes that frightened Pat. She was too apprehensive to risk a question as to the outcome, and simply stared at him with wide, fearful, questioning eyes.
“I called up your home,” he said irrelevantly. “I told them you left with me early this morning. Your mother’s still in bed, although it’s after ten.” He paused. “Slip in without anyone seeing you, will you, Honey? And rumple up your bed.”
“If I haven’t lost my key,” she said, still with the question in her eyes.
“It’s in the mailbox. Magda found it on the porch this morning. I talked to her.”
She could bear the uncertainty no longer. “Tell me!” she demanded.

===Chinese===
“现在完了吗？”医生终于再次现身时，帕特颤声问。这没完没了的等待把她磨得更加憔悴，苍白的脸上刻着紧张劳累的痕迹。
“二十分钟前就完了，”霍克说。他的脸上也带着紧张的痕迹；不但如此，他眼里还有一种困惑游疑的神情，吓着了帕特。她太惶恐，不敢冒险询问结果，只是瞪大那双惊惧探问的眼睛望着他。
“我往你家挂了电话，”他没头没脑地说。“我告诉他们，你今天一早就跟我出门了。你母亲还在床上，虽然已过了十点。”他顿了顿。“悄悄溜进去，别让任何人看见，行吗，亲爱的？再把你的床弄乱。”
“只要我的钥匙没丢，”她说，眼里仍悬着那个疑问。
“在信箱里。今早玛格达在门廊上捡到的。我跟她交代过了。”
这份悬心她再也熬不住了。“告诉我！”她要求道。

===Original===
“It’s all right, I think.”
“You mean﻿—he’ll live?”
The Doctor nodded. “I think so.” He turned his puzzled eyes on her.
“Oh!” breathed Pat. “Thank God!”
“You wanted him back, Honey, didn’t you?” Horker’s tone was gentle.
“Oh, yes!”

===Chinese===
“没事了，我想。”
“你是说——他能活了？”
医生点点头。“我想是。”他把那双困惑的眼睛转向她。
“哦！”帕特长出一口气。“感谢上帝！”
“你要他回来，亲爱的，不是吗？”霍克的语气温和。
“哦，是的！”

===Original===
“Devil and all?”
“Yes﻿—devil and all!” she echoed. Suddenly she sensed something strange in the other’s manner. She perceived the uncertainty in his visage, and felt a rising trepidation. “What’s the matter?” she queried anxiously. “You’re not telling me everything! Tell me, Dr. Carl!”
“There’s something else,” he said. “I’m not sure, Pat, but I think﻿—I hope﻿—you’ve got him back without the devil!”
“He’s cured?” Her voice was incredulous; she did not dare accept the Doctor’s meaning.
“I hope so. At least I located the cause.”
“What was it?” she demanded, an unexpected vigor livening her tired body. “What was that devil? Tell me! I want to know, Dr. Carl!”

===Chinese===
“连魔鬼一起？”
“是的——连魔鬼一起！”她应和道。突然，她觉出对方神色有异。她看出他脸上的游疑，一阵不安油然而起。“怎么了？”她急切地问。“你没把一切都告诉我！告诉我，卡尔医生！”
“还有别的情况，”他说。“我不敢肯定，帕特，但我想——我希望——你接回的他，已经不带魔鬼了！”
“他痊愈了？”她的声音满是不敢相信；她不敢接受医生的意思。
“我希望如此。至少我找到病根了。”
“是什么？”她追问道，一股意想不到的活力振奋起疲惫的身体。“那魔鬼到底是什么？告诉我！我要知道，卡尔医生！”

===Original===
“I think the best name for it is a tumor,” he said slowly. “I told them in there it was a tumor. I wish I knew myself.”
“A tumor! I don’t understand!”
“I don’t either, Pat﻿—not fully. It’s something on or beyond the border of medical knowledge. I don’t think any living authority could classify it definitely.”
“But tell me!” she cried fiercely. “Tell me!”
“Well, Honey﻿—I’ll try.” He paused thoughtfully. “Cancers and tumors﻿—sarcomas﻿—are curious things, Dear. Doctors aren’t at all sure just what they are. And one of their peculiarities is that they sometimes seem to be trying to develop into separate entities, trying to become human by feeding like parasites on their hosts. Do you understand?”
“No,” said the girl. “I’m sorry, Dr. Carl, but I don’t.”

===Chinese===
“我想，它最贴切的名字是肿瘤，”他缓缓地说。“我在里头跟他们说的就是肿瘤。我自己也真想知道它算什么。”
“肿瘤！我不明白！”
“我也不明白，帕特——不完全明白。它处在医学知识的边界上，或者干脆在界外。我想当今世上没有哪位权威能给它一个明确的分类。”
“可你得告诉我！”她激烈地喊。“告诉我！”
“好吧，亲爱的——我试试。”他若有所思地停了停。“癌和肿瘤——肉瘤——是些古怪东西，亲爱的。医生们根本拿不准它们究竟是什么。它们的怪处之一，是有时它们仿佛在设法长成独立的个体，像寄生虫一样靠宿主喂养，设法变成人。你听懂了吗？”
“没有，”姑娘说。“对不起，卡尔医生，我不懂。”

===Original===
“I mean,” he continued, “that sometimes these growths seem to be trying to develop into﻿—into organisms. I’ve seen them, for instance﻿—every surgeon has﻿—with bones developing. I’ve seen one with a rather perfect jawbone, and little teeth, and hair. As if,” he added, “it were making a sort of attempt to become human, in a primitive, disorganized fashion. Now do you see what I mean?”
“Yes,” said the girl, with a violent shudder. “Dr. Carl, that’s horrible!”
“Life sometimes is,” he agreed. “Well,” he continued slowly, “I opened up our patient’s skull at the point where the fluoroscope indicated the bullet. I trephined it, and there, pierced by the shot, was this﻿—” He hesitated, “﻿—this tumor.”
“Did you﻿—remove it?”
“Of course. But it wasn’t a natural sort of brain tumor, Honey. It was a little cerebrum, apparently joined to a Y-shaped branch of the spinal cord. A little brain, Pat﻿—no larger than your small fist, but deeply convoluted, and with the pre-Rolandic area highly developed.”
“What’s pre-Rolandic, Dr. Carl?” asked Pat, shivering.

===Chinese===
“我是说，”他接着讲，“这些增生物有时似乎在设法长成——长成有机体。比如我见过——哪个外科医生都见过——里面长出骨头的。我见过一个，长着相当完整的下颌骨，还有小牙齿，还有头发。仿佛，”他补充道，“它在以某种原始而紊乱的方式，尝试变成人。现在你明白我的意思了吗？”
“明白了，”姑娘说，猛地打了个寒战。“卡尔医生，这太可怕了！”
“生命有时就是这么可怕，”他附和道。“是这样，”他缓缓地继续说，“我在荧光屏指示子弹的位置打开了咱们病人的颅骨。我做了环钻，就在那儿，被子弹击穿的，就是这个——”他迟疑了一下，“——这个肿瘤。”
“你——把它切除了？”
“当然。可它不是寻常那种脑瘤，亲爱的。它是一个小小的大脑，显然连在脊髓一条Y形的分支上。一颗小小的脑子，帕特——不比你攥紧的小拳头大，但沟回深深盘绕，中央前区还高度发达。”
“什么叫中央前区，卡尔医生？”帕特问，身子发抖。

===Original===
“The seat of the motor nerves. The home, you might say, of the will. This brain was practically all will﻿—and I wonder,” he said musingly, “if that explains the ungodly, evil fascination the creature could command. A brain that was nothing but pure willpower, relieved by its parasitic nature of all the distractions of a directing body! I wonder﻿—” He fell silent.
“Tell me the rest!” she said frantically.
“That’s all, Honey. I removed it, and I guess I’m the only surgeon in the world who ever removed a brain from a human skull without killing the patient! Luckily, he had two of them!”
“Oh God!” murmured the girl faintly. She turned to Horker. “But he will live?”
“I think so. Your shot killed the devil, it seems.” He frowned. “I said it was a tumor; I told them it was a tumor, but I’m not sure. Perhaps, just as some people are born with six fingers or toes on each member, he was born with two brains. It’s possible; one developed normally, humanly, and the other﻿—into that creature we faced last night. I don’t know!”
“It’s what I said,” asserted Pat. “It’s a devil, and what you’ve just told me about tumors proves it. They’re devils, that’s all, and some day some student is going to cut one loose and raise it to maturity outside a human body, and you’ll see what a devil is really like! And go ahead and laugh!”

===Chinese===
“运动神经的中枢。也可以说，是意志的老巢。这颗脑子几乎全是意志——我在想，”他沉吟道，“这是不是就解释了那东西能施展的那种邪门、邪恶的魅惑。一颗除了纯粹意志再无其他的脑子，又因寄生的本性，免去了指挥一具躯壳的一切牵累！我想知道——”他沉默了。
“把剩下的告诉我！”她发急地说。
“就这些了，亲爱的。我把它切了下来，而且我大概是世上唯一一个从人的颅骨里取出一颗脑子、却没有弄死病人的外科医生！幸好，他长了两颗！”
“哦，上帝！”姑娘虚弱地喃喃。她转向霍克。“可他能活？”
“我想能活。你那一枪，看来打死的是魔鬼。”他皱起眉。“我说了它是肿瘤；我跟他们说的是肿瘤，可我自己没把握。也许，就像有人每只手脚生来六指一样，他生来就有两颗脑子。这有可能；一颗发育得正常、有人性，另一颗——发育成了我们昨夜面对的那个东西。我不知道！”
“这正是我说过的，”帕特断言。“它就是魔鬼，你方才讲的关于肿瘤的一切恰恰证明了这一点。它们就是魔鬼，仅此而已；早晚有一天，哪个学者会切下一个来，在人体之外把它养大，到那时你们就知道魔鬼真正的模样了！尽管笑吧！”

===Original===
“I’m not laughing, Pat. I’d be the last one to laugh at your theory, after facing that thing last night. It had satanic powers, all right﻿—that paralyzing fascination! You felt it too; it wasn’t just a mental lapse on my part, was it?”
“I felt it, Dr. Carl! I’d felt it before that; I was always helpless in the presence of it.”
“Could it,” he asked, “have imposed its will actively on yours? I mean, could it have made you actually do what it asked there at the end, just before I recovered enough sense to let out that bellow?”
“To take off﻿—my dress?” She shivered. “I don’t know, Dr. Carl.﻿—I’m afraid so.” She looked at him appealingly. “Why did I yield to it so?” she cried. “What made me find such a fierce pleasure in its kisses﻿—in its blows and scratches, and the pain it inflicted on me? Why was that, Dr. Carl?”
“Why,” he countered, “do gangsters’ girls and apache women enjoy the cruelties perpetrated on them by their men? There’s a little masochism in most women, and that﻿—creature was sadistic, perverted, abnormal, and somehow dominating. It took an unfair advantage of you, Pat; don’t blame yourself.”
“It was﻿—utterly evil!” she muttered. “It was the ultimate in everything unholy.”

===Chinese===
“我没有笑，帕特。昨夜面对过那东西之后，天下最后一个该笑你这理论的人才是我。它有撒旦的力量，这没错——那种令人瘫软的魅惑！你也感受到了；那总不至于只是我一时的神志失常吧？”
“我感受到了，卡尔医生！在那之前我就感受过；一在它面前，我总是身不由己。”
“它有没有可能，”他问，“把它的意志能动地强加于你的意志之上？我是说，在我恢复神智、发出那声吼之前的一刻，它是不是真能让你照它的要求去做？”
“脱掉——我的衣服？”她一颤。“我不知道，卡尔医生。——恐怕是能。”她哀恳地望着他。“我为什么那样顺从它？”她喊道。“是什么让我在它的吻里——在它的殴打和抓挠里，在它加于我的疼痛里，尝到那样一种凶猛的快感？为什么会那样，卡尔医生？”
“那你说说，”他反问，“匪徒的马子和阿帕契女郎们，怎么会在自家男人施加的暴虐里寻到乐趣？大多数女人身上都有一点受虐倾向，而那个——东西施虐、变态、反常，又带着某种主宰之力。它占了你的不公平的便宜，帕特；别责怪自己。”
“它——邪恶透顶！”她喃喃道。“它是一切不神圣之物的极致。”

===Original===
“It was an aberrant brain,” said Horker. “You can’t judge it by human standards, since it wasn’t actually human. It was, I suppose, just what you said﻿—a devil. I didn’t even keep it,” he added grimly. “I destroyed it.”
“Do you know what it meant by saying it was a question of synapses?” she asked.
“That was queer!” The Doctor’s voice was puzzled. “That remark implies that the thing itself knew what it was. How? It must have possessed knowledge that the normal brain lacked.”
“Was it a question of synapses?”
“In a sense it was. The nerves from the two rival brains must have met in a synaptic juncture. The oftener the aberrant brain gained control, the easier it became for it to repeat the process, as the synapse, so to speak, wore thin. That’s why the attacks intensified so horribly toward the end; the habit was being formed.”
“Last night was the very worst!”

===Chinese===
“它是一颗畸变的脑子，”霍克说。“你没法用人的标准去评判它，因为它实际上不是人。它，我猜，正如你所说——是个魔鬼。我连它都没留，”他冷冷地补了一句。“我把它毁了。”
“它说那是突触的问题，你知道是什么意思吗？”她问。
“这才叫怪！”医生的语声困惑不解。“那句话表明，那东西自己知道它是什么。怎么知道的？它必定拥有那颗正常脑子所没有的知识。”
“那真是突触的问题吗？”
“从某种意义上说，是的。两颗争权的脑子发出的神经，必然在一处突触接点会合。畸变的脑子每夺得一次控制，再夺一次就容易些，可以说，突触被磨得越来越薄。所以到了后期，发作才恶化得那么可怕；习性正在养成。”
“昨夜那次是最厉害的！”

===Original===
“Of course. As the thing itself pointed out, I made the mistake of drugging the normal brain and giving the other complete control of the body. At other times, there’d always been the rivalry to weaken whichever was dominant.”
“Does that mean,” asked Pat anxiously, “that Nick’s character will be changed now?”
“I think so. I think you’ll find him less meek, less gentle, than heretofore. More spirited, perhaps, since his energies won’t be drained so constantly by the struggle.”
“I don’t care!” she said. “I’d like that, and anyway, it doesn’t make a bit of difference to me as long as he’s just﻿—*my* Nick.”
The Doctor gave her a tender smile. “Let’s go home,” he said, pinching her cheek in his great hand.
“Can you leave him?”

===Chinese===
“当然。正如那东西自己指出的，我错在药倒了正常的脑子，把肉体的完全控制权拱手让给了另一颗。换作平时，两脑相争，总有一方被削弱，无论当权的是哪个。”
“那是不是意味着，”帕特焦急地问，“尼克的性格从此就变了？”
“我想是的。我想你会发现他比从前少了几分温顺、几分柔和。也许更有锐气，因为他的精力不会再被那场争斗无休止地抽干了。”
“我不在乎！”她说。“我倒喜欢那样；反正，只要他还是——*我的*尼克，别的对我没有半点分别。”
医生给了她一个温柔的笑。“回家吧，”他说，用那只大手捏了捏她的脸颊。
“你能撇下他？”

===Original===
“I’ll run back after a while, Honey. I think he’ll do.” He took her hand, drawing her after him. “Don’t forget to slip in unseen, Pat, and rumple up your bed.”
“Rumple it!” She gave him a weary smile. “I’ll be *in* it!”
“Good idea. You look a bit worn out, Honey, and we can’t have you getting sick now, or even pull a temporary faint like that one last night.”
“I didn’t faint!”
“Maybe not,” grinned Horker. “Perhaps the proceedings grew a little boring, and you just lay down on the couch for a nap. It *was* a dull evening.”

===Chinese===
“过一会儿我再跑回来一趟，亲爱的。我看他不会有事。”他牵起她的手，带她往外走。“别忘了悄悄溜进去别让人看见，帕特，再把床弄乱。”
“弄乱它！”她给他一个疲惫的笑。“我要*躺*在里头！”
“好主意。你看着有点累坏了，亲爱的，咱们现在可不能让你病倒，连昨晚那种一时晕厥也不能再来一回。”
“我没有晕！”
“也许没有，”霍克咧嘴一笑。“大概是场面有点乏味了，你不过是躺到沙发上打个盹。昨晚*确实*无趣。”

## II Science of Mind / 二 心灵的科学
===Original===
“She’s out,” said Pat as the massive form of Dr. Carl Horker loomed in the doorway. “Your treatments must be successful; Mother’s out playing bridge.”
The Doctor gave his deep, rumbling chuckle. “So much the better, Pat. I don’t feel professional anyway.” He moved into the living room, depositing his bulk on a groaning davenport. “And how’s yourself?”
“Too well to be a patient of yours,” retorted the girl. “Psychiatry! The new religion! Just between friends, it’s all applesauce, isn’t it?”
“If I weren’t trying to act in place of your father, I’d resent that, young lady,” said the Doctor placidly. “Psychiatry is a definite science, and a pretty important one. Applied psychology, the science of the human mind.”
“If said mind exists,” added the girl, swinging her slim legs over the arm of a chair.
“Correct,” agreed the Doctor. “In my practice I find occasional evidence that it does. Or did; your generation seems to have found substitutes.”

===Chinese===
“她出门了，”卡尔·霍克医生魁伟的身躯出现在门口时，帕特说道。“你的治疗想必大见成效；母亲打桥牌去了。”
医生发出低沉滚雷般的轻笑。“那更好，帕特。反正我也不在行医状态。”他走进客厅，把那副身躯安顿到一张吱呀抗议的长沙发上。“你自己怎么样？”
“好得很，轮不到当你的病人，”姑娘回敬道。“精神病学！新式宗教！朋友之间说句实话——全是胡扯，不是吗？”
“要不是我在努力代行你父亲的职责，这话我可要见怪了，小姐，”医生平心静气地说。“精神病学是一门确凿的科学，还挺要紧。应用心理学，研究人类心灵的科学。”
“前提是那颗心灵存在，”姑娘补了一句，把纤细的腿翘上椅子扶手。
“没错，”医生附和。“我在行医中偶尔能找到它存在的证据。或者说曾经存在；你们这一代似乎找到了替代品。”

===Original===
“Which appears to work just as well!” laughed Pat. “All our troubles are more or less inherited from your generation.”
“Touche!” admitted Dr. Horker. “But my generation also bequeathed you some solid values which you don’t know how to use.”
“They’ve been weighed and found wanting,” said Pat airily. “We’re busy replacing them with our own values.”
“Which are certainly no better.”
“Maybe not, Doc, but at least they’re ours.”
“Yours and Tom Paine’s. I can’t see that you young moderns have brought any new ideas to the social scheme.”

===Chinese===
“而且用起来一样趁手！”帕特笑道。“我们的一切烦恼，多少都是从你们那代继承来的。”
“击中要害！”霍克医生认了。“可我们那代也给你们传下了一些扎实的价值，只是你们不会用。”
“上过秤了，分量不足，”帕特轻飘飘地说。“我们正忙着用自家的价值取而代之。”
“那些肯定好不到哪儿去。”
“也许不好，医生，可至少是我们自己的。”
“你们的，也是汤姆·潘恩的。我看不出你们这些时髦年轻人给社会格局带来过什么新点子。”

===Original===
“New or not, we’re the first ones to give ’em a tryout. Your crowd took it out in talk.”
“That’s an insult,” observed the Doctor cheerfully. “If I weren’t acting *in loco parentis*﻿—”
“I know! You’d give me a few licks in the spot popularly supposed to do the most good! Well, that’s part of a parent’s privilege, isn’t it?”
“You’ve grown beyond the spanking age, my dear. Physically, if not mentally﻿—though I don’t say the process would hurt me as much as you. I’d doubtless enjoy it.”
“Then you might try sending me to bed without my dinner,” the girl laughed.
“That’s a doctor’s prerogative, Pat. I’ve even done that to your Mother.”

===Chinese===
“新也罢不新也罢，我们是头一批拿来做试验的。你们那伙人只落得空谈。”
“这可是人身攻击，”医生乐呵呵地说。“要不是我在以*代理家长*的身份行事——”
“我知道！你就要在公认最有疗效的那块地方给我几下巴掌！嗯，这也是做家长的特权之一，不是吗？”
“你早过了挨揍的年纪，亲爱的。肉体上如此——心智上就难说了——不过我并不是说，这过程会像伤你一样伤我。毫无疑问我会很享受。”
“那你可以试试罚我不许吃晚饭就上床，”姑娘笑道。
“那是医生的特权，帕特。我对你母亲都使过这一手。”

===Original===
“In other words, you’re a complete flop as a parent. All the responsibilities, and none of the privileges.”
“That expresses it.”
“Well, you elected yourself, Doc. It’s not my fault you happened to live next door.”
“No. It’s my misfortune.”
“And I notice,” remarked Pat wickedly, “that you’re not too thoroughly *in loco* to neglect sending Mother a bill for services rendered!”
“My dear girl, that’s part of the treatment!”

===Chinese===
“这么说，你当家长当得一败涂地。责任全担了，特权一样没捞着。”
“正是这么回事。”
“可这是你自封的，医生。谁叫你偏偏住在隔壁，可怨不着我。”
“是啊。这是我的不幸。”
“而且我注意到，”帕特不怀好意地说，“你*代理*归代理，给母亲寄诊费账单这事倒一点没耽误！”
“亲爱的姑娘，那是治疗的一部分！”

===Original===
“So? And how?”
“I furnish a bill just steep enough to keep your mother from indulging too frequently in medical services. Without that little practical check on her inclinations, she’d be a confirmed neurotic. One of those sweet, resigned, professional invalids, you know.”
“Then why not send her a bill tall enough to cure her altogether?”
“She might change to psychoanalysis or New Thought,” chuckled the Doctor. “Besides, your father wanted me to look after her, and besides that, I like having the run of the house.”
“Well, I’m sure I don’t mind,” observed Pat. “We’ve a dog and a canary bird, too.”
“You’re in fine fettle this afternoon!” laughed her companion. “Must’ve been a successful date last night.”

===Chinese===
“是吗？怎么讲？”
“我开的账单，数目刚好陡峭到能拦住你母亲，不让她太纵情享用医疗服务。若没有这道对付她嗜好的小小现实闸门，她早成铁定的神经质了。就是那种甜美、认命、职业化的病人，你懂的。”
“那干嘛不给她开一张高到能把她彻底治好的账单？”
“她没准就改投精神分析或新思想运动了，”医生轻笑。“再说，你父亲托我照看她；再再说，我也喜欢在这座宅子里随意进出。”
“嗯，我当然不介意，”帕特说。“我们家还养了条狗、一只金丝雀呢。”
“今天下午你精神头十足！”她的同伴笑道。“昨晚的约会想必大获成功。”

===Original===
“It was.” Her eyes turned suddenly dreamy.
“You’re in love again, Pat!” he accused.
“Again? Why the ‘again’?”
“Well, there was Billy, and that Paul﻿—”
“Oh, those!” Her tone was contemptuous. “Merely passing fancies, Doc. Just whims, dreams of the moment﻿—in other words, puppy love.”
“And this? I suppose this is different﻿—a grand passion?”

===Chinese===
“正是。”她的眼睛忽然变得如梦似幻。
“你又恋爱了，帕特！”他指控道。
“又？干吗说‘又’？”
“这个，有过比利，还有那个保罗——”
“哦，那些呀！”她的口气充满不屑。“一时的心血来潮罢了，医生。不过是些念想、当场的梦——说白了，小狗恋情。”
“那这次呢？我猜这次不一样——是场轰轰烈烈的激情？”

===Original===
“I don’t know,” she said, frowning abruptly. “He’s nice, but﻿—odd. Attractive as﻿—well, as the devil.”
“Odd? How?”
“Oh, he’s one of those minds you think we moderns lack.”
“Intellectual, eh? New variety for you; out of the usual run of your dancing collegiates. I’ve often suspected that you picked your swains by the length and lowness of their cars.”
“Maybe I did. That was one of the chief differences between them.”
“How’d you meet this mental paragon?”

===Chinese===
“我不知道，”她说，蓦地皱起眉。“他很好，可是——古怪。迷人得——嗯，迷人得像魔鬼。”
“古怪？怎么个古怪法？”
“哦，他是你认定我们时髦青年最缺的那种头脑。”
“智识型的，嗯？对你来说是新品种；跟你那些跳舞的大学生不是一个路数。我常疑心，你挑情郎是按他们车身的长度和底盘高低来的。”
“也许吧。那本来就是他们之间的主要差别之一。”
“这位智力楷模你是怎么碰上的？”

===Original===
“Billy Fields dragged him around to one of those literary evenings he affects﻿—where they read Oscar Wilde and Eugene O’Neil aloud. Bill met him at the library.”
“And he outshone all the local lights, I perceive.”
“He surely did!” retorted Pat. “And he hardly said a word the whole evening.”
“He wouldn’t have to, if they’re all like Billy! What’s this prodigy’s specialty?”
“He writes. I think﻿—laugh if you want to!﻿—I think perhaps he’s a genius.”
“Well,” said Doctor Horker, “even that’s possible. It’s been known to occur, but rarely, to my knowledge, in your generation.”

===Chinese===
“比利·菲尔兹硬把他拽去参加了一场他惯常操办的那种文学晚会——大家轮流高声念奥斯卡·王尔德和尤金·奥尼尔。比尔是在图书馆认识他的。”
“于是他盖过了当地所有的名流，我明白了。”
“他可不就是！”帕特反唇相讥。“而且整晚他几乎一句话没说。”
“要是那些人都跟比利一个样，他本来也用不着开口！这位奇才的专长是什么？”
“他写作。我想——想笑就笑吧！——我想他说不定是个天才。”
“这个嘛，”霍克医生说，“连那也是可能的。据我所知这种事有过先例，只是在你们那一代，罕有。”

===Original===
“Oh, we’re just dimmed by the glare of brilliance from yours.” She swung her legs to the floor, facing the Doctor. “Do you psychiatrists actually *know* anything about love?” she queried.
“We’re supposed to.”
“What is it, then?”
“Just a device of Nature’s for perpetuating the species. Some organisms manage without it, and do pretty well.”
“Yes. I’ve heard references to the poor fish!”
“Then they’re inaccurate; fish have primitive symptoms of eroticism. But below the vertebrates, notably in the amoeba, I don’t recall any amorous habits.”

===Chinese===
“哦，我们不过是被你们那代的光辉照得黯淡了。”她把腿转回地板，面对医生。“你们这些精神科医生，当真*懂得*爱情吗？”她问。
“理论上应该懂。”
“那它是什么？”
“不过是自然用来延续物种的一个机关。有些生物没有它也过得下去，还过得挺好。”
“是啊。那些可怜的鱼的典故我听过！”
“那他们说得不对；鱼是有原始的爱欲征兆的。但再往下，脊椎动物以下，尤其是变形虫，我不记得它们有什么谈情说爱的习性。”

===Original===
“Then your definition doesn’t explain a thing, does it?”
“Not to one of the victims, perhaps.”
“Anyway,” said Pat decisively, “I’ve heard of the old biological urge before your kind analysis. It doesn’t begin to explain why one should be attracted to this person and repelled by that one. Does it?”
“No, but Freud does. The famous Oedipus Complex.”
“That’s the love of son for mother, or daughter for father, isn’t it? And I don’t see how that clears up anything; for example, I can just barely remember my father.”
“That’s plenty. It could be some little trait in these swains of yours, some unimportant mannerism that recalls that memory. Or there’s that portrait of him in the hall﻿—the one under the mellow red light. It might happen that you’d see one of these chaps under a similar light in some attitude that brings the picture to mind﻿—or a hundred other possibilities.”

===Chinese===
“那你这个定义什么也没解释清楚，对吗？”
“对当事的受难者而言，或许没有。”
“反正，”帕特斩钉截铁地说，“你这套高见之前，那套老掉牙的生物冲动我早听过了。它压根解释不了，为什么这个人让你动心，那个人让你反胃。对吧？”
“解释不了，可弗洛伊德解释得了。大名鼎鼎的俄狄浦斯情结。”
“就是儿子恋母、女儿恋父那一套，对不对？我看不出这能澄清什么；比如说，我对我父亲只剩下模模糊糊的印象。”
“那就足够了。可能是你这些情郎身上的某种小特质，某种无足轻重的小癖好，唤起了那段记忆。要么就是门厅里他那张画像——柔和红灯底下的那张。说不定哪次，你在类似的灯光下看见这些小子中的某一个，摆出个让画像浮上心头的姿势——或者还有一百种别的可能。”

===Original===
“Doesn’t sound entirely convincing,” objected Pat with a thoughtful frown.
“Well, submit to the proper treatments, and I’ll tell you exactly what caused each and every one of your little passing fancies. You can’t expect me to hit it first guess.”
“Thanks, no! That’s one of these courses where you tell the doctor all your secrets, and I prefer to keep what few I have.”
“Good judgment, Pat. By the way, you said this chap was odd. Does that mean merely that he writes? I’ve known perfectly normal people who wrote.”
“No,” she said, “it isn’t that. It’s﻿—he’s so sweet and gentle and manageable most of the time, but sometimes he has such a thrilling spark of mastery that it almost scares me. It’s puzzling but fascinating, if you grasp my import.”
“Huh! He’s probably a naturally selfish fellow who’s putting on a good show of gentleness for your benefit. Those flashes of tyranny are probably his real character in moment of forgetfulness.”

===Chinese===
“听着不全然可信，”帕特蹙眉沉思着反对。
“这样吧，你接受正规治疗，我就能一五一十告诉你，你每一次小小的心血来潮各是何因。总不能指望我一猜即中。”
“多谢，不必！那号疗程得把秘密向医生和盘托出，而我仅剩的这几桩，还想留着自己收着。”
“有主见，帕特。对了，你说这小子古怪。是不是仅仅因为他写作？写作的人里完全正常的，我见识过。”
“不，”她说，“不是那个。是——他大多时候那么温存、和气、好驾驭，可有时候，他身上会迸出那么一星主宰的火花，惊心动魄，几乎吓着我。这事费解，却迷人，你要明白我的意思。”
“嗐！他八成天生是个自私的家伙，特意为你演一出温文尔雅的好戏。那几阵霸道的闪光，多半是他忘形时露出的真性情。”

===Original===
“You doctors can explain anything, can’t you?”
“That’s our business. It’s what we’re paid for.”
“Well, you’re wrong this time. I know Nick well enough to know if he’s acting. His personality is just what I said﻿—gentle, sensitive, and yet﻿—It’s perplexing, and that’s a good part of his charm.”
“Then it’s not such a serious case you’ve got,” mocked the doctor. “When you’re cool enough to analyze your own feelings, and dissect the elements of the chap’s attraction, you’re not in any danger.”
“Danger! I can look out for myself, thanks. That’s one thing we mindless moderns learn young, and don’t let me catch you puttering around in my romances! *In loco parentis* or just plain loco, you’ll get the licking instead of me!”
“Believe me, Pat, if I wanted to experiment with affairs of the heart, I’d not pick a spitfire like you as the subject.”

===Chinese===
“你们这些医生，什么都解释得了，对吧？”
“那是我们的本行。拿人钱财，替人解惑。”
“可惜这回你错了。我对尼克知之甚深，他是不是演戏我看得出来。他这个人正如我所说——温存、敏感，然而——让人捉摸不透，而这正是他魅力的一大部分。”
“那你的病症不算重，”医生嘲道。“当你还够冷静，能分析自己的感情，解剖那小子吸引力的成分，你就还没到危险的份上。”
“危险！我自己会照看自己，多谢。这是我们这些没脑子的时髦青年从小就学会的本事之一，还有，别让我撞见你在我的罗曼史里瞎搅和！*代理家长*也好，干脆*发疯*也罢，挨揍的会是你，不是我！”
“相信我，帕特，我要真想做情感实验，绝不会挑你这么个炮仗脾气当实验品。”

===Original===
“Well, Doctor Carl, you’re warned!”
“This Nick,” observed the Doctor, “must be quite a fellow to get the princess of the North Side so het up. What’s the rest of his cognomen?”
“Nicholas Devine. Romantic, isn’t it?”
“Devine,” muttered Horker. “I don’t know any Devines. Who are his people?”
“Hasn’t any.”
“How does he live? By his writing?”

===Chinese===
“那么，卡尔医生，你可是受到警告了！”
“这个尼克，”医生评道，“能把北区的公主撩得这么心火旺，必是个人物。他的全名叫什么？”
“尼古拉斯·德文。够浪漫吧？”
“德文，”霍克喃喃道。“我不认识哪家德文。他家里是干什么的？”
“没有家人。”
“他靠什么生活？靠写作？”

===Original===
“Don’t know. I gathered that he lives on some income left by his parents. What’s the difference, anyway?”
“None. None at all.” The other wrinkled his brows thoughtfully. “There was a colleague of mine, a Dr. Devine; died a good many years ago. Reputation wasn’t anything to brag about; was a little off balance mentally.”
“Well, Nick isn’t!” snapped Pat with some asperity.
“I’d like to meet him.”
“He’s coming over tonight.”
“So’m I. I want to see your mother.” He rose ponderously. “If she’s not playing bridge again!”

===Chinese===
“不知道。我琢磨他靠父母留下的一点进项过活。这又有什么分别？”
“没有。半点分别没有。”对方若有所思地皱起眉。“我从前的同行里有位德文医生；过世好些年了。名声谈不上光彩；精神上有点失衡。”
“反正尼克不失衡！”帕特带着几分尖刻厉声说。
“我倒想会一会他。”
“他今晚要来。”
“我也来。我要看看你母亲。”他沉重地站起身。“可别又在打桥牌！”

===Original===
“Well, look him over,” retorted Pat. “And I think your knowledge of love is a decided flop. I think you’re woefully ignorant on the subject.”
“Why’s that?”
“If you’d known anything about it, you could have married mother some time during the last seventeen years. Lord knows you’ve tried, and all you’ve attained is the state of *in loco parentis* instead of *parens*.”

===Chinese===
“行啊，尽管把他瞧个够，”帕特回敬。“还有，我看你的爱情学问是彻底砸了锅。你在这门学问上无知得可怜。”
“此话怎讲？”
“你要真懂一点，过去这十七年里，早该把我母亲娶回家了。天知道你试了多少回，到头来混成的，不过是*代理家长*，而非*家长*本尊。”

## XXIX Scopolamine for Satan / 二十九 给撒旦的东莨菪碱
===Original===
The glass was struck from Pat’s hand, and the water-clear contents streamed into pools and darkening blots over the table and its litter of papers. She stared unseeingly at the mess, without realizing that it was Nick who had dashed the draft from her very lips. She felt neither anger nor relief, but only a numbness, and a sense of anticlimax. Somewhere below the bell was ringing again, and a door was resounding to violent blows, but she only continued her bewildered, questioning gaze.
“I can’t let you, Pat!” he muttered, answering her unspoken query.
“But Nick﻿—why?”
“There’s somebody at the door, isn’t there? Mustn’t we find out who?”
“What difference can it make?” she asked wearily.
“I don’t know. I want to find out.”

===Chinese===
那杯药从帕特手里被打飞，清水般的液体泼洒开来，在桌上那堆凌乱的纸件间积成一汪汪、洇出一团团深色的渍。她视而不见地盯着那片狼藉，还没意识到正是尼克把那杯死亡从她唇边击落。她不觉得愤怒，也不觉得宽慰，只有一种麻木，和一种虎头蛇尾的空落。楼下某处门铃又响了，一扇门正被擂得山响，可她只是继续那茫然探问的凝望。
“我不能让你这么做，帕特！”他喃喃道，回答了她没问出口的疑问。
“可是尼克——为什么？”
“门口有人，不是吗？咱们不该弄清楚是谁吗？”
“那又能有什么分别？”她倦怠地问。
“我不知道。我想弄清楚。”

===Original===
“It’s that illusion of hope again,” she murmured. “That’s all it is, Nick﻿—and it means now that it’s all to do over again! The whole thing, from the beginning﻿—and we were so near﻿—the end!”
“I know,” he said miserably. “I know all that, but﻿—” He paused as the insistent racket below was redoubled. “I’m going to answer that bell,” he ended.
He moved away from her, vanishing through the room’s single door. She watched his disappearance without moving, but no sooner had he passed from sight than a curious feeling of fear oppressed her. She cast off the numbness and languor, and darted after him into the darkness of the hall.
